## Monologue

I’ll begin with a story.

At five each morning, before the buses started and before the bakery switched on its ovens, Mara unlocked the little station at the end of the line.

There had not been a train for eleven years.

She still swept the platform. She still checked the clock against her watch. And on wet mornings, she still set a yellow sign beside the entrance, warning passengers that the floor might be slippery.

People in the town had stopped asking why.

At first they had been sympathetic. Then practical. Then, for a little while, unkind. Eventually, as often happens with things that refuse to change, they had become fond of her.

“There goes the stationmaster,” they would say, watching her bicycle rattle over the cobbles.

Mara had never been the stationmaster. She had sold tickets.

The distinction mattered to her.

A stationmaster might have had authority over a closure. A ticket seller had only a drawer, a stamp, and instructions.

On the morning the stranger arrived, the clock above the platform was three minutes slow.

Mara stood on a chair beneath it, turning the minute hand with one finger.

“You’ll make someone miss their train doing that,” a voice said.

She looked down.

The stranger carried a brown suitcase with a broken clasp. He wore a dark coat too warm for the weather, and his shoes were clean in a way that suggested he had not walked from anywhere nearby.

“There are no trains,” Mara said.

“Then I’ve arrived early.”

“Eleven years early, or eleven years late.”

He considered this.

“Which would you recommend?”

Mara climbed down from the chair.

“Neither. The bus stop is outside the library.”

“I’m not taking a bus.”

“Most people say that the first time.”

The stranger smiled, but not as if she had made a joke. More as if he had recognized a tune.

He crossed to the ticket window and set his suitcase at his feet.

“One to Bellwether,” he said.

Mara felt the small, familiar room become unfamiliar around her.

Bellwether was the stop beyond the mountain.

Bellwether was the reason the line had closed.

She placed both hands flat on the counter.

“That station no longer exists.”

“I know.”

“The track is gone.”

“Some of it.”

“The tunnel collapsed.”

“Yes.”

She waited for him to explain himself. He did not.

Outside, the first bakery van rolled past the square, its sides silver in the thin morning light.

“Who are you?” she asked.

The man reached into his coat and took out a ticket.

Not a new one. It was soft at the corners, folded once through the middle. The ink had faded to the color of old tea.

Mara recognized the punch mark before she recognized her own handwriting.

A small crescent, clipped from the upper edge.

She had made that mark on the last day.

“I think,” the stranger said gently, “you may have sold me a return.”

Mara did not take the ticket.

On the last day, there had been rain. Not remarkable rain, not at first. Ordinary rain that darkened coats and made people impatient. She remembered a woman holding a newspaper over a sleeping child. A boy dragging a wooden horse with one wheel missing. A man at the window asking whether the weather would delay the train.

She remembered saying, “Not this little bit.”

For eleven years she had tried not to remember saying it.

“What is your name?” she asked.

“Elias Vale.”

She looked at his face more carefully.

Time had touched it, but lightly. A little gray at the temples. A small scar beside his mouth. He might have been forty. He might have been younger when she last saw him.

If she had last seen him.

“There was a list,” she said.

“I expect there was.”

“I read it.”

He waited.

“I read it many times.”

“I’m sorry.”

That unsettled her more than anything else.

She turned away and opened the drawer beneath the counter. The old timetable lay inside, under a packet of rubber bands and a screwdriver with a blue handle. She knew where it was without looking.

When she unfolded it, the paper made a dry, brittle sound.

“Bellwether,” she said. “Five forty-two. Arrives six seventeen.”

Elias glanced at the clock.

It read five thirty-nine.

Mara looked at her watch.

Five forty-two.

She had not finished setting the clock.

From somewhere beyond the platform came a sound she knew so well that, for a moment, she did not hear it at all.

A rail settling under weight.

Then another.

Mara walked past the ticket window, through the waiting room, and out onto the platform.

The tracks were there.

They lay between the weeds as if they had never been lifted: two dark lines curving toward the cutting, their upper surfaces bright in the early light.

At the far end of the platform, the signal had risen.

Behind her, Elias picked up his suitcase.

“Will it stop?” he asked.

Mara could not answer.

A breeze moved through the waiting room. Somewhere inside, loose paper fluttered. The station clock gave a little click.

Then, beyond the bend, a whistle sounded.

Not loud. Not mournful. Just a driver announcing an approach.

Mara gripped the edge of the door.

The locomotive came into view slowly, black paint dulled by rain that was not falling here. Behind it were three passenger carriages. Their windows held the pale reflection of a sky clouded over in some other place.

She knew the number on the engine.

She knew the dent in the second carriage.

She knew, as the train drew alongside, the woman standing at the third window with one hand raised.

Mara’s sister had always waved too early.

Even when the train was still moving. Even when there was no chance Mara would miss her. She would spot the station building and lift her hand as if arriving were a kind of rescue.

Mara took one step forward.

The brakes sighed.

For a while, neither sister moved.

Then the carriage door opened, and Ada stepped down onto the platform with a small overnight bag.

“You’ve cut your hair,” Ada said.

Mara put a hand to her head.

It had been short for seven years.

Ada looked around the station. Her gaze rested on the peeling bench, the clean windows, the yellow warning sign beside the door.

“You kept it open.”

“No.”

Ada looked at her.

“I kept opening it,” Mara said.

Ada nodded, accepting the correction.

Behind them, Elias approached the conductor. The two exchanged a few words too quietly for Mara to hear. The conductor checked the old ticket, made a mark with a silver punch, and stepped aside.

Elias lifted his suitcase onto the train.

“You’re leaving?” Mara asked him.

He turned.

“I said it was a return.”

“But you came from—”

“Yes.”

He glanced toward Ada, then back at Mara.

“Sometimes the order gets confused.”

The conductor raised his watch.

Ada saw it.

“I haven’t much time,” she said.

Mara felt the years gathering in her throat, all the things she had prepared to say in dreams and forgotten upon waking.

“I told a man the rain wouldn’t delay it.”

Ada’s expression changed, not with surprise but with attention.

“At the window,” Mara said. “He asked, and I said—”

“You didn’t know.”

“I should have.”

“How?”

Mara looked down the train, toward the curve and the mountain beyond it.

“I don’t know.”

Ada set her bag on the platform.

“Mara.”

“The timetable said—”

“Mara.”

She stopped.

Ada reached for her hands. Hers were warm.

“There were people whose job it was to watch the mountain,” Ada said. “There were people whose job it was to inspect the tunnel. There were people who could have stopped that train. You sold a man a ticket.”

Mara tried to pull her hands away, but Ada held them.

“And you were wrong about the rain,” Ada said. “You were wrong. That is all you were.”

The station clock clicked again.

From inside the carriage came the low murmur of passengers. Someone laughed, briefly, at something Mara could not see.

“Can you stay?” she asked.

Ada’s eyes filled.

“No.”

“Can I come?”

“No.”

Mara nodded once, though she had not meant to.

Ada reached into her coat pocket and brought out a little paper bag.

“Mrs. Bell had these on the train,” she said. “She insisted I take two.”

Inside were barley sweets, dusted white.

The sight of them undid Mara.

She cried awkwardly, with her mouth open, trying to draw a breath that would not come. Ada held her. The conductor turned his back and studied the signal.

For eleven years, Mara had imagined grief as a room she could leave if only she found the right door.

Now, standing on the platform, she understood nothing of the kind. She only felt her sister’s sleeve beneath her fingers and the hard shapes of the sweets pressed between their palms.

At last Ada stepped away.

“Go somewhere tomorrow,” she said.

“Where?”

“Anywhere that isn’t here.”

Mara gave a wet, disbelieving laugh.

“You always did give terrible directions.”

“And you always wanted a timetable.”

The conductor cleared his throat.

Ada picked up her bag.

Mara watched her climb into the carriage. At the window, her sister raised her hand too early.

This time Mara raised hers at once.

The train pulled away.

For a moment, Elias stood at the last window, his brown suitcase visible on the rack behind him. He touched two fingers to the brim of a hat he was not wearing, then seemed amused at himself.

The carriages rounded the bend.

The whistle sounded once.

And the tracks were gone.

Mara remained on the platform until the bakery van came back through the square.

Then she went inside.

The clock read six seventeen.

She left it alone.

That afternoon she swept the waiting room, emptied the little bin beside the bench, and washed the cup she kept under the counter. She put the old timetable back in the drawer.

On a scrap of paper she wrote:

Closed tomorrow.

She considered it, then added:

Please use the bus stop outside the library.

The next morning, the baker saw her standing there with a small blue bag.

“Station shut?” he called from his van.

“For the day.”

“Where are you off to?”

The bus was turning into the square.

Mara reached into her pocket. There was one barley sweet left, its paper twisted tightly at both ends.

“I’ll know when I get there,” she said.

---

By the time the bus reached the river road, Mara had discovered three things.

First, she disliked sitting at the front.

Second, a ticket printed by a machine felt disappointingly light.

Third, she had not been farther than the next town in eleven years, and the world had taken this as permission to rearrange itself.

There was a supermarket where the old cattle market had been. The petrol station had become a place that sold plants in glazed pots. Beyond the bridge, houses stood in a field where she remembered sheep.

She turned the barley sweet over in her pocket but did not unwrap it.

At the fifth stop, a girl boarded carrying a violin case and a shoe box.

The bus was nearly empty. Naturally, the girl sat beside Mara.

“Would you mind holding this?” she asked, offering the shoe box.

“What’s in it?”

“A bird.”

“Alive?”

“Yes.”

“Then I might mind.”

“It won’t come out.”

Mara accepted the box.

There were air holes in the lid. Something inside shifted with the delicate, determined scrape of claws against cardboard.

“What happened to it?”

“Window.”

“Yours?”

“The school’s.”

“Shouldn’t you be there?”

“I’m taking it to someone.”

“The bird or the violin?”

“The bird.”

The girl leaned the violin case against her knees. A strand of hair had escaped her braid and was caught in her mouth; she blew it away with visible irritation.

Mara looked at the box.

“Does this someone know about birds?”

“He says he does.”

“That’s not always enough.”

“He’s my grandfather.”

Mara considered the likelihood that this answered her concern.

The bus crossed the river. Sunlight broke between the warehouses and flashed on the water.

“My name’s Nell,” the girl said.

“Mara.”

“Are you going to the hospital?”

“No.”

“People with bags like that usually are.”

Mara looked at her blue overnight bag. It had belonged to her mother and possessed the particular brownish handles of an object that had spent decades being almost replaced.

“I’m going somewhere for the day.”

“Where?”

“I haven’t decided.”

Nell regarded her with interest.

“Are you running away?”

“No.”

“That’s what I’d say.”

The bird moved again.

Mara held the box more steadily.

At the next stop an elderly man got on with an empty shopping trolley. He greeted the driver by name and paid with so many coins that the driver had to close his hand twice to hold them.

“Morning, Nell,” he said as he passed.

“Morning.”

He sat at the back.

“Does everyone know you?” Mara asked.

“Not everyone.”

“Does your school?”

Nell looked out the window.

Mara sighed.

“I sold tickets,” she said.

“What?”

“At a railway station. People used to tell me things. Or not tell me things. You begin to recognize both.”

Nell picked at a split in the cardboard lid.

“There’s an assembly.”

“And?”

“I’m supposed to play.”

“The violin.”

“Yes.”

“Is the bird injured?”

“Yes.”

“So you found it.”

“Yes.”

“And now you’re taking it to your grandfather instead of playing at the assembly.”

Nell nodded.

“How convenient,” Mara said.

The girl’s ears turned red.

For a few minutes they rode in silence.

Then Nell said, “Convenient for the bird, too.”

Mara smiled before she could stop herself.

“Very possibly.”

Outside, the road narrowed. The bus passed a row of cottages, then a garage with its doors open and no one inside. Beyond that, fields rose toward a long line of beech trees.

“Where does your grandfather live?”

“Marsh End.”

“Is that the last stop?”

“Nearly.”

Mara watched the countryside unfold.

“Then I might go there.”

Nell turned quickly.

“I’m not trying to steal anything.”

“I didn’t say you were.”

“Or trick you.”

“I was holding a box when we established our acquaintance. If anyone has been tricked, the evidence is fairly clear.”

Nell gave a small laugh.

The bird scratched once against the lid.

Mara looked down at it.

“How far did it fall?”

“Not far. It hit the glass and landed in the flower bed.”

“And you couldn’t leave it.”

“No.”

Mara nodded.

She understood that much.

At Marsh End, the driver opened the doors beside a stone shelter filled with old leaves.

Nell stood and reached for the shoe box.

“I’ll carry it,” Mara said.

The girl looked at her.

“You have the violin,” Mara added.

They walked down a lane between high hedges. Somewhere nearby, a dog barked with the exhausted dedication of a creature performing a duty no one appreciated.

At the end of the lane stood a low house with a green door.

A man was kneeling in the front garden, arguing with a rosebush.

“You can’t have all of it,” he told the plant.

“Grandad,” Nell called.

He looked up.

The first thing Mara noticed was the dirt on his face.

The second was his expression when he saw the box.

“Oh, Nell.”

“It hit a window.”

He rose carefully, one hand against his knee.

“And what did the window do to you?”

“Nothing.”

“Then why aren’t you at school?”

Nell set her violin case on the path.

The man glanced at Mara.

“Accomplice?”

“Transport,” she said.

“Unwitting?”

“Initially.”

He wiped his hands on his trousers and held them out for the box.

“Let’s see.”

Inside, huddled against a crumpled scarf, was a small brown bird with a yellowish breast. Its beak opened as the light fell over it, but it made no sound.

The man bent close.

“Don’t poke it,” Nell said.

“I wasn’t planning to.”

“You look as if you might.”

“I have many expressions. You mistake them.”

He carried the box toward the house.

“Tea?” he asked over his shoulder.

Mara hesitated.

She had planned nothing beyond leaving. Now that she had done it, the day appeared to contain an alarming amount of room.

“Yes,” she said. “Thank you.”

The kitchen was full of things in the process of being repaired. A clock without a face occupied one corner of the table. Beside it lay a toaster, two wooden chair legs, and a radio whose open back revealed a density of wires Mara found faintly indecent.

“Sit anywhere that isn’t sharp,” the man said.

Nell moved the clock onto a windowsill.

“This is Mara.”

“Jonah.”

He gave a nod, then disappeared through a side door with the bird.

Nell put the kettle on.

“Does he repair birds?” Mara asked.

“Sometimes.”

“What about the other times?”

“He calls someone who does.”

“That sounds sensible.”

“He hates being called sensible.”

“Then we shan’t tell him.”

Nell found three mugs that appeared to have survived unrelated disasters.

When Jonah returned, he was carrying the empty box.

Nell’s face changed.

“It’s all right,” he said quickly. “I’ve put it somewhere quiet.”

“Will it live?”

“I don’t know yet.”

“Do you think it will?”

“I think we’ll give it the chance.”

Nell looked as if she might object to this answer, then lowered her eyes to the kettle.

Jonah sat opposite Mara.

“Where did she find you?”

“On the bus.”

“A known habitat for useful strangers.”

“I was warned there might be a bird.”

“That was decent of her.”

He looked at Nell.

“School rang.”

She stood very still.

“I told them you were here.”

“You didn’t know I was coming.”

“No. But I know where you come.”

The kettle clicked off.

Nell poured the water too quickly, then spent a long time finding a cloth to wipe the counter.

Jonah let her.

Mara looked at the radio.

“Do you mend all these things?”

“Most of them.”

“Does anyone pay you?”

“Occasionally they threaten to.”

“And the rest?”

“They bring eggs. Jam. More broken things.”

“An expanding enterprise.”

“Disastrous business model.”

He turned one of the mugs so its handle faced her.

“What did you do?”

“Sold railway tickets.”

“Hereabouts?”

“At Larkspur.”

His hands stopped moving.

Mara noticed.

“So you know it,” she said.

“I know the station.”

“Most people do.”

“Yes.”

Nell set the milk between them.

For a moment, only the cooling kettle made a sound.

Then Jonah said, “I worked signals.”

Mara looked up.

“Where?”

He did not answer at once.

He did not need to.

Outside, something small landed on the kitchen windowsill, tapped once against the glass, and flew away.

Nell watched the two adults.

“What?” she asked.

Neither spoke.

“What is it?”

Jonah folded his hands.

“The Bellwether line,” he said.

Mara’s chair scraped against the floor.

She had stood before she knew she meant to.

“I should go.”

Nell looked from one to the other.

“But the bus isn’t—”

“I’ll walk.”

Jonah did not stop her.

That made it worse, though she could not have said why.

At the door, she found she still held her mug. She went back and set it down with such care that a little tea spilled over the rim.

“I’m sorry,” he said.

She looked at him then.

It was an ordinary face. Tired around the eyes, unshaven beneath the chin. There was a small pale patch on one hand where a ring had been.

She had imagined other faces over the years.

People had jobs. People had names.

Until this moment, she had preferred the first.

“Were you on duty?” she asked.

“Yes.”

Nell took one step backward, toward the counter.

Jonah’s gaze stayed on Mara.

“I sent the warning,” he said. “Too late.”

“How late?”

“Four minutes.”

Mara closed her eyes.

Four minutes was a useful length of time. You could sell three tickets. Make tea. Open a window that had stuck in its frame.

You could turn a clock’s hand and think nothing of it.

“They told us the message wasn’t received,” she said.

“It wasn’t. The line went down.”

“But you sent it.”

“Yes.”

“Too late.”

“Yes.”

She opened her eyes.

He was not defending himself.

That, too, was worse.

Nell’s voice came quietly from the counter.

“Grandad was the one who went up there.”

Jonah turned.

“Nell.”

“He did.”

“Nell.”

“After.”

Mara looked at his hands again.

There were scars across the knuckles, pale and ridged.

“I read the reports,” she said.

Jonah nodded.

“I don’t remember your name.”

“I know.”

The room seemed suddenly too warm.

Mara sat down.

No one moved for a while.

Then Jonah reached for the milk and asked, “Would you like fresh tea?”

She almost laughed. It was such a small question to set before the rest of it.

“Yes,” she said.

Nell let out a breath.

As Jonah stood, Mara put her hand into her pocket and found the barley sweet.

She held it for a moment.

Then she placed it on the table between them.

Neither Jonah nor Nell asked where it had come from.

---

The bird was alive at noon.

Jonah checked it without bringing it into the kitchen, and when he returned Nell examined his face before he spoke.

“Still resting,” he said.

“That’s not an answer.”

“It’s the answer I have.”

She looked away.

He put bread, cheese, and three tomatoes on the table. Lunch required a knife to be retrieved from a drawer that would only open if someone lifted the toaster first.

Mara lifted the toaster.

“Thank you,” Jonah said.

“It’s heavier than it looks.”

“Most broken things are.”

He glanced at her, then shook his head.

“That sounded wise. I apologize. Usually I’m only talking about machinery.”

Mara cut the bread.

After they had eaten, Jonah telephoned the school from the hall. He spoke quietly, with the door half closed.

Nell sat at the kitchen table, staring at her violin case.

“Do you play well?” Mara asked.

“Sometimes.”

“Is that what you were afraid of?”

Nell frowned.

“What?”

“That today wouldn’t be one of the times.”

For a while the girl said nothing.

Then she opened one clasp on the case.

“My hand shakes.”

“Always?”

“Only when people are looking.”

“That must make it difficult.”

“Everyone says it stops once you start.”

“Does it?”

“No.”

Mara thought about the crowded platform on the last morning. The questions. The certainty people expected from a uniform and a printed schedule.

“People like to give you the answer they wish were true,” she said.

Nell opened the second clasp.

“My mother says I don’t have to be perfect.”

“That sounds reasonable.”

“She says it the way you’d say, ‘Don’t drop the plates.’”

Mara smiled.

From the hallway came Jonah’s voice, briefly louder.

“Yes. I understand.”

Nell shut the case.

“Am I in trouble?”

“Probably,” Mara said.

The girl looked at her with offended surprise.

“I don’t see how a bird cancels everything.”

“I thought you were on my side.”

“I’m on the bird’s side. Its position is very clear.”

Nell tried not to smile and failed.

Jonah returned.

“Your mother’s collecting you at three.”

Nell groaned.

“The school would like a note.”

“You can write one.”

“I intend to.”

“What will it say?”

“‘Nell found an injured bird and made several additional decisions.’”

Mara laughed.

It surprised her enough that she stopped.

Jonah pretended not to notice.

In the afternoon, he took them through the garden to a small wooden shed. There were tools on the walls and shallow drawers with labels written in a hand so careful it looked printed.

The bird’s box stood on a bench beneath a shaded window.

Jonah lifted the lid.

The bird was upright.

Nell gripped Mara’s sleeve.

It turned its head, quick and indignant, and hopped into a corner.

“That’s better,” Jonah said.

“Can we let it out?”

“Not in here.”

He carried the box into the garden.

They stood near the hedge, away from the house. Jonah lowered it onto the grass and folded back the lid.

Nothing happened.

The bird remained in the corner, looking from one enormous creature to another.

“Does it know?” Nell whispered.

“It knows the lid is open.”

“Should we tip it?”

“No.”

They waited.

From the road came the sound of a tractor changing gear. A bee moved methodically through the lavender beside the shed.

The bird hopped onto the edge.

Nell’s fingers tightened.

It balanced there, feathers stirring.

Then it flew.

Not beautifully. Not at first. One wing dipped; it dropped toward the grass, caught itself, and rose into the hedge.

Nell made a small sound.

Jonah stood very still.

From inside the leaves came a rustle, then another. The bird appeared on a higher branch.

It looked back once.

Or seemed to.

Then it was gone.

Nell released Mara’s sleeve.

“I knew it,” she said.

“No, you didn’t,” Jonah said.

“No.”

He picked up the empty box.

“But I’m glad you hoped.”

At three, Nell’s mother arrived in a small car with a cracked rear light and an expression prepared for several possible arguments.

She hugged her daughter before beginning any of them.

Mara liked her for that.

Nell put the violin on the back seat, then returned to the garden gate.

“Will you come again?” she asked.

Mara looked at Jonah.

He was standing by the shed with the folded box beneath his arm.

“I don’t know,” she said.

Nell considered this answer with the seriousness she had given the bird.

“All right.”

Then she held out her hand.

Mara shook it.

It was absurdly formal, and neither of them laughed.

When the car had gone, the garden seemed much larger.

“The bus leaves at twenty past,” Jonah said.

“I know.”

“Of course.”

Mara looked toward the lane.

“I could make another pot.”

She turned back.

Jonah did not add anything.

After a moment she said, “I still don’t know what to say to you.”

“Nor I to you.”

“I was angry.”

“I know.”

“I am angry.”

He nodded.

She sat on the bench beside the back door.

“Tea, then.”

He went inside.

Mara watched a patch of hedge that now contained no visible bird at all.

---

On the return journey, the blue bag occupied the seat beside her.

Inside it were two tomatoes, a repaired torch she had not asked for, and a note for Nell’s school that Jonah had accidentally placed with Mara’s belongings.

She discovered it halfway home.

To whom it may concern, it began.

Nell was absent this morning because she found an injured bird.

There were several lines below, crossed out so firmly that the paper had nearly torn.

The final sentence read:

We have discussed the difference between doing a kind thing and being excused from everything else.

Underneath, in smaller writing:

She will return tomorrow.

Mara folded the note and put it back in the bag.

At Larkspur, the bus dropped her outside the library. The square was busy with the evening’s errands. A dog slept beneath the butcher’s window. Someone had chalked an advertisement for strawberries on the pavement, and people were carefully walking around the letters as though they were wet.

The station stood at the far end of the square.

She could see her notice on the door.

For a moment she considered taking it down.

Instead, she went home.

Her house smelled faintly of lavender polish and closed rooms. She opened the kitchen window, set the tomatoes on the sill, and searched a drawer for the telephone directory.

Jonah’s surname was on the school note.

She found his number on the second attempt.

He answered after five rings.

“I’ve got your letter,” she said.

There was a pause.

“Ah.”

“I could post it.”

“You could.”

“The post here has already gone.”

“It can wait a day.”

“Yes.”

She looked out the window. In the next garden, her neighbor was pinning a sheet to the line, shaking it once before reaching for the pegs.

“I’ll bring it tomorrow,” Mara said.

“All right.”

Neither hung up.

Then Jonah said, “Bring the torch if it doesn’t work.”

She glanced at it on the table.

“Did you put batteries in?”

“No.”

“Then how will I know whether it works?”

“That,” he said, “is one of the difficulties with giving things away.”

After the call, she cut one of the tomatoes.

It was warm from the bus and red all the way through.

She ate it standing at the counter, with salt from the palm of her hand.

The next morning, at five, she woke as usual.

She lay still.

A cart passed in the street. Pipes clicked in the wall. Somewhere a door closed with the particular care of a person trying not to wake a household.

Mara watched the pale rectangle of the window.

At half past five she got up.

She dressed, made tea, and put on her coat.

The key turned in the station lock as it always had.

Inside, the waiting room was cool.

Nothing had changed.

The bench stood beneath the timetable board. The ticket window reflected a thin bar of light. Behind the counter, the old drawer waited with its stamps and rubber bands.

She walked out onto the platform.

There were no rails.

Grass filled the bed of the line, jeweled with dew. Beyond the bend, the cutting was thick with brambles.

The clock read five forty-two.

It had stopped.

Mara stood beneath it for a long time.

Then she brought out the chair.

She climbed up, opened the glass face, and removed the key from its hook inside the case.

The mechanism resisted at first. She had not wound it herself in years; that had been one of the jobs she paid a man from the next town to do, because she preferred the clock’s difficulties to remain someone else’s responsibility.

She turned the key.

Once.

Twice.

At the third turn, the ticking began.

She set the hands to the time on her watch.

Six minutes past six.

Then she put the chair away.

On the door, beneath yesterday’s notice, she added a second line.

Open Saturday, ten until noon.

She went to fetch a broom.

There was a great deal to do, if she was only going to be here two hours a week.

---

By Saturday, the town had formed four theories.

The first was that Mara was ill.

The second was that she had come into money.

The third was that the council had finally interfered.

The fourth, which spread most quickly, was that she had met someone on the bus.

The baker arrived at ten carrying a paper bag.

“I’m not asking,” he said.

“Good.”

“People are saying things.”

“They generally do.”

He put the bag on the counter.

“Two currant buns.”

“I only ordered one.”

“You didn’t order either.”

He looked around the waiting room.

Mara had moved the old timetable to the wall beside the ticket window. In its place on the large board was a sheet of clean paper.

At the top she had written:

WHERE WOULD YOU LIKE TO GO?

Beneath it, pinned in two neat rows, were current bus timetables.

The baker leaned closer.

“Are you selling tickets?”

“No.”

“Then what are you doing?”

“Finding out.”

He considered this.

“Coast,” he said.

“What?”

“You asked.”

Mara picked up a pencil.

“Which part?”

“Any bit with a bench and something fried.”

At ten fifteen, Mrs. Bell arrived.

Not the Mrs. Bell from the train. Her niece, who had inherited the house, the name in local conversation, and a determined belief that public buildings should contain flowers.

She placed a vase on the windowsill.

“For the station.”

“It isn’t a station.”

Mrs. Bell looked at the platform.

“It’s making a convincing effort.”

By half past ten, there were six people in the waiting room.

One wanted to know the cheapest way to visit a brother three counties away. One had mistaken the opening hours for an announcement of a jumble sale and refused to accept that there might not be one. An elderly woman sat on the bench without asking for anything.

Mara let her.

At eleven, Jonah appeared in the doorway with a cardboard box.

Mara looked at it.

“No birds.”

“Clock parts.”

“Is that better?”

“Less likely to fly.”

Behind him stood Nell, violin case in hand.

Mara leaned on the counter.

“Shouldn’t you be somewhere?”

“It’s Saturday.”

“A useful defense.”

Nell came inside.

Jonah set the box down.

“You said the clock was sticking.”

“I said it had stopped.”

“That’s a particularly committed form of sticking.”

He looked up at it.

“May I?”

Mara handed him the key.

While Jonah fetched the chair, Nell opened her violin case.

The room quieted in a way that made her shoulders rise.

“You don’t have to,” Mara said.

“I know.”

Nell tightened the bow.

She stood beside the window where the light fell across the flowers.

The first note was thin.

The second broke.

Nell stopped.

No one spoke.

Outside, the baker lifted a crate from his van and set it down with a thud.

Nell looked at Mara.

Mara did not smile encouragingly. She did not tell her to take a breath or pretend nobody was there.

She waited.

Nell put the bow to the strings again.

This time the tune continued.

It was simple, and she played it slowly. Her hand shook. Everyone could see it.

Halfway through, the elderly woman on the bench closed her eyes.

Jonah remained on the chair, one hand resting against the open clock.

When Nell finished, the room stayed quiet for a heartbeat.

Then Mrs. Bell said, “Again, if you don’t mind.”

Nell looked down at her violin.

“All right.”

---

The station acquired a kettle because Mrs. Bell objected to the absence of one.

It acquired cups because the baker’s wife objected to paper.

It acquired a shelf of books because someone left a box beneath the bench, and a sign reading PLEASE TAKE ONE because no one otherwise would.

Mara was careful about the changes.

“No committee,” she told Jonah.

He was repairing the loose leg of the bench.

“I haven’t proposed one.”

“You looked thoughtful.”

“I was wondering where this screw came from.”

“No minutes. No constitution. No one saying ‘moving forward’ when they mean next week.”

“An ambitious charter.”

She gave him a look.

He held up the screw in surrender.

The town council sent a letter inquiring about public liability insurance.

Mara read it twice.

Then she took it to the woman in the council office who had once bought four return tickets to the seaside and wept because her husband could no longer travel.

They had a practical conversation.

The following Saturday, the station acquired public liability insurance.

It did not acquire a committee.

In June, Nell played at the school concert.

Mara and Jonah sat in the third row.

Before going onstage, Nell looked through the curtain and found them. She seemed briefly annoyed to discover that they were real.

Her mother leaned toward Mara.

“She’s been practicing every evening.”

“I thought she was trying to avoid it.”

“She is. The practicing is part of the campaign.”

Onstage, Nell adjusted the music stand three times.

Then she began.

Her hand shook.

The tune continued.

Afterward, Jonah gave her a small packet wrapped in newspaper.

Inside was a brass door knocker shaped like a bird.

Nell stared at him.

“What am I supposed to do with this?”

“Knock on doors.”

“We have a bell.”

“Then your options are extensive.”

She rolled her eyes and hugged him.

Mara gave her a packet of barley sweets.

Nell opened it immediately.

“Why these?”

“My sister liked them.”

“Does she live far away?”

Mara looked at the little twists of paper.

“Yes,” she said.

Nell held the packet toward her.

Mara took one.

---

By August, people were calling the station the waiting room.

Mara found she preferred it.

A station promised a departure. A waiting room asked less.

On Saturdays, people came with destinations, problems, spare courgettes, and things they had meant to say to someone for a long time. Mara could usually help with the first. The courgettes she distributed aggressively.

The problems were more complicated.

She learned not to answer too quickly.

Once, a young man asked whether he should leave town.

“Do you want to?” she said.

“I don’t know.”

“Then we’ve established where to start.”

He looked disappointed.

She made him tea.

Jonah came most weeks, sometimes with Nell, sometimes alone. He repaired the latch on the lavatory door and refused to discuss payment until Mara pointed out that she was perfectly capable of making him unwelcome.

He named a price.

She paid it.

Neither of them apologized.

One rainy Saturday, after everyone else had gone, they sat beneath the clock with two untouched cups between them.

“I saw Ada,” Mara said.

Jonah did not move.

She had not planned to tell him.

The rain tapped against the windows.

“My sister,” she added.

“I know who she was.”

Mara looked down.

“Of course.”

“Where did you see her?”

“Here.”

“When?”

“The day before I met you.”

He waited.

“She came on the train.”

The clock ticked.

Mara had expected doubt. She had also expected, less comfortably, belief that was really kindness.

Jonah gave her neither.

“What did she say?”

Mara held the cup with both hands.

“That I was wrong about the rain.”

He looked toward the platform.

Then she told him the rest.

Not perfectly. She stopped several times, corrected the order, left out the barley sweets and then went back for them.

Jonah listened.

When she finished, the tea was cold.

At last he said, “I used to think I heard the signal bell.”

“When?”

“At night. For years.”

She looked at him.

“Sometimes I’d get as far as the back door before I remembered I didn’t work there anymore.”

“Do you still?”

“Not often.”

He rubbed a thumb along the edge of his cup.

“Perhaps I’d like to.”

Mara nodded.

That she understood.

Outside, rainwater ran along the platform edge and disappeared into the weeds.

“Would you believe me?” she asked.

Jonah took a little time.

“I believe you saw her.”

It was not quite what she had wanted.

It was better than being reassured.

She stood and carried the cups to the sink.

Behind her, the clock gave a faint, uneven click.

Jonah looked up.

“That,” he said, “I can do something about.”

---

In September, they took the bus to the coast.

The baker came because it had been his idea. Mrs. Bell came because someone needed to bring napkins. Nell came with her mother and complained that organized outings were for old people, then fell asleep against the window before the first stop.

Mara carried her blue bag.

Jonah carried nothing, which she regarded as suspicious.

“What if it rains?”

“I’ll get wet.”

“What if you’re hungry?”

“There are shops.”

“What if they’re closed?”

“Then we’ll have discovered a very unusual coast.”

The baker sat behind them, defending the precise conditions under which fish and chips could be considered breakfast.

At the seaside, the wind was fierce.

Mrs. Bell’s hat escaped within thirty seconds.

Nell caught it against a railing, and the baker declared this evidence that young people remained useful despite reports to the contrary.

They found a bench overlooking the beach.

The tide was out. Children in rolled trousers pursued the sea across the sand as though it were retreating from them personally.

Mara sat.

For a long time she said nothing.

Jonah sat beside her.

After a while, he opened his hand.

Two barley sweets lay in his palm.

She looked at them.

“Shop by the bus stop,” he said.

“I know.”

She chose one.

The paper crackled between her fingers.

Below them, Nell was drawing a line in the sand with a stick. Her mother stood nearby, shoes in one hand, watching the horizon.

The baker appeared carrying a parcel warm enough to steam through its paper.

“Something fried,” he announced.

Mrs. Bell produced the napkins.

The wind took the first one.

They watched it lift, turn, and settle beyond the railing.

“Well,” Mrs. Bell said, reaching for another. “There’s always one.”

Mara laughed.

No one looked surprised this time.

---

Autumn changed the platform before it changed the square.

The first leaves gathered in the track bed, caught among the grasses where rails had once run. Mara cleared the drains and left the rest.

She had begun to dislike making everything look as though nothing had happened.

Inside, she hung a photograph beside the timetable.

Ada stood on the platform in a summer dress, her eyes narrowed against the sun. One hand was raised to shade her face.

For years Mara had kept the photograph in a drawer because it seemed unbearable that the person in it did not know.

Now she placed it where the morning light could reach it.

Mrs. Bell noticed at once.

“Your sister?”

“Yes.”

“She had a lovely smile.”

“She was laughing at my hat.”

“What was wrong with it?”

“Nothing.”

Mrs. Bell studied the picture.

“I see.”

Mara glanced at her.

“It was a perfectly good hat.”

“I’m sure.”

That Saturday, a man came in looking for train times.

He was young, with a backpack and a phone displaying a map that had apparently retained the station with professional confidence.

“I’m sorry,” Mara said. “There hasn’t been a service here for years.”

He looked through the window at the platform.

“Oh.”

“The bus goes from outside the library.”

“Right.”

She found the timetable and showed him.

He frowned.

“That won’t get me there in time.”

“No.”

“Is there another way?”

Mara looked at the map on his phone.

There was, but it involved a different bus, a short walk, and a connection that allowed only twelve minutes.

“I can show you,” she said. “But I can’t promise you’ll make it.”

He listened carefully.

When she finished, he said, “Do you think I should try?”

Once, she would have answered too quickly.

Now she slid a piece of paper toward him and wrote down the times.

“I think you should know what trying involves.”

He took it.

“Thanks.”

After he had gone, Mara stood at the window and watched him cross the square.

She hoped he would make it.

She let that be all.

---

The first hard frost came in November.

Jonah arrived early and found Mara on the platform, testing the old water tap with a kettle of warm water.

“You’ll crack it,” he said.

“I won’t.”

“You might.”

“So might leaving it frozen.”

He considered this.

“Fair.”

He took the kettle from her and poured slowly over the pipe.

Their breath hung in the air.

From somewhere beyond the cutting came a metallic clink.

Mara straightened.

Jonah stopped pouring.

They listened.

The sound came again.

Not a bell. Not wheels.

A farmer was repairing a gate in the field beyond the hedge.

Mara let out a breath.

Jonah set down the kettle.

“Did you think—”

“Yes.”

He nodded.

They stood together for another moment before going inside.

Neither mentioned it again that day.

But the following Saturday, Jonah brought a small tin.

“What’s that?” Mara asked.

“Biscuits.”

“What’s wrong with them?”

“Nothing.”

“Then why the tin?”

“To hold the biscuits.”

She opened it.

Beneath a folded cloth lay a signal bell, polished but scarred along one edge.

She looked up.

“I thought you might know what to do with it,” he said.

Mara touched the rim.

It made no sound.

“Where has it been?”

“My shed.”

“All this time?”

“Yes.”

She closed the lid.

“Are you sure?”

“No.”

That was the right answer.

She set the tin on the shelf behind the counter.

“We needn’t decide today.”

Jonah sat down.

“No.”

Mara put on the kettle.

Outside, the frost began to melt, darkening the stones one by one.

---

In December, the waiting room stayed open until four.

This had happened without a meeting, a vote, or any other event Mara could reasonably have opposed.

Someone had suggested lights.

Someone else had brought a tree.

The baker had announced that closing at noon in the presence of mince pies would be a failure of leadership.

“I’m not the leader,” Mara said.

Everyone had behaved as though she were being modest.

By three, the windows were dark mirrors.

Nell stood beside the tree, tuning her violin. She had grown taller since spring, a change Mara found both ordinary and faintly unreasonable.

Jonah was arguing with a string of lights.

“They were working at home.”

“So were you,” Mara said.

He looked at her.

“Cruel.”

“Accurate.”

The room filled slowly.

People brought damp coats and cold hands. The young man who had considered leaving town came with a woman Mara had not met. Mrs. Bell distributed plates. The baker produced something he insisted was a traditional recipe and would not identify more precisely.

Nell played.

Her hand still shook a little.

Mara noticed because she knew to look.

During the second tune, she went behind the counter and took down the tin.

She opened it.

The bell lay in its cloth.

For a moment she could see Jonah’s kitchen as it had been the first day: the clock without a face, the open radio, the shoe box on the table.

She lifted the bell.

It was heavier than she expected.

Jonah looked up from the lights.

Their eyes met.

Mara carried it to the wall beside the door, where a bare hook had held nothing for years.

“Will it fit?” she asked.

Jonah came over.

He examined the hook, then the bell.

“Yes.”

He fastened it carefully.

No one else paid much attention. Mrs. Bell was trying to persuade the baker to reveal whether his contribution contained almonds.

Mara took the small handle.

Jonah stood beside her.

She rang it once.

The note was clear and surprisingly light.

The room quieted.

Mara had not prepared a speech.

This was unfortunate, because everyone was now looking at her as if she had.

She put down her hand.

“Tea’s ready,” she said.

The baker applauded.

Everyone laughed, and the room resumed.

Jonah looked at the bell.

“How was that?”

Mara listened to the last vibration fade.

“All right,” she said.

Then, because it was true, she added, “It was all right.”

---

After Christmas, snow closed the road to Marsh End.

Nell telephoned to report that her grandfather was being foolish.

“In what way?” Mara asked.

“He says the path doesn’t need clearing because no one should be walking on it.”

“A coherent position.”

“He walked on it.”

“That weakens the argument.”

“He’s listening.”

“Good.”

There was a muffled exchange.

Then Jonah came on the line.

“I haven’t fallen.”

“An admirable record. Do you intend to preserve it?”

“I only went to the shed.”

“Was the shed coming here?”

“No.”

“Then perhaps it could wait.”

He made a noise that might have been laughter or disagreement.

Mara looked out at her own path.

It was white all the way to the gate.

For the first time in years, she did not think immediately of the station.

She thought of the kettle, the telephone, and the fact that there were two portions of soup in her freezer.

“I’ll call tomorrow,” she said.

“Mind the ice.”

“I’m not going anywhere.”

“Good.”

She sat by the kitchen window with her tea.

Across the street, a child was attempting to make a snowman from snow too powdery to hold together. He pressed a handful into place. It fell apart. He tried again.

After a while, his older sister came out carrying a watering can.

Mara watched them confer.

Then she laughed into her cup.

---

The thaw brought a letter addressed to THE PERSON WHO HELPED ME WITH THE BUS.

Inside was a photograph of a newborn baby.

On the back, the young traveler from autumn had written:

I made it with forty minutes to spare.

Her name is Rose.

Thank you for not telling me it was impossible.

Mara read the message several times.

Then she pinned the photograph to the board.

When Jonah arrived, he stood before it.

“Someone you know?”

“Not really.”

He read the back of the card where she had left it folded open.

“Forty minutes,” he said.

“Yes.”

“That’s a useful amount of time.”

Mara looked at him.

He was still studying the photograph.

“Yes,” she said. “It is.”

That afternoon, she found him on the platform, looking toward the mountain.

The bare branches gave a clearer view than summer had.

She stood beside him.

For a while they said nothing.

Then Jonah said, “I’ve been asked to speak.”

“Where?”

“The memorial. This year.”

Mara felt herself grow still.

“Will you?”

“I don’t know.”

“What would you say?”

He put his hands in his coat pockets.

“What happened.”

“They know what happened.”

“Some of it.”

The wind moved along the platform.

Mara thought of the old reports. Names that had become duties. Duties that had become a single terrible morning.

“I could come,” she said.

He turned.

“You don’t have to.”

“I know.”

He nodded once.

Then they went inside, because it was cold and the waiting room was open.

---

On the anniversary, Mara did not unlock the station at five.

She woke, looked at the window, and remained in bed until the sky began to lighten.

At seven, she made tea.

At eight, she took Ada’s photograph from the wall and cleaned the glass.

At nine, Jonah arrived.

He wore a dark suit that did not quite fit his shoulders and a tie Nell had evidently chosen.

“She said the other one made me look apologetic.”

“What does this one make you look?”

“Uncomfortable.”

Mara straightened it.

“There.”

“Better?”

“More symmetrical.”

They walked to the memorial.

It stood at the edge of the square: a low stone, the names cut into its face. People had left flowers beneath it. Rainwater rested in the carved letters.

Nell and her mother were already there.

Mrs. Bell stood with a bunch of white chrysanthemums wrapped in brown paper. The baker had closed his shop for an hour.

When Jonah’s turn came, he unfolded one sheet of paper.

His hands were steady until he began.

Then they were not.

Mara watched him.

He told them where he had been.

He told them what he had seen on the instruments, when he had sent the warning, and how long afterward the line had gone silent.

He did not simplify it.

He did not ask anyone for forgiveness.

At one point he stopped and looked down at the paper.

Mara thought he might be unable to continue.

Nell moved closer, though she did not touch him.

Jonah took a breath.

Then he finished.

Afterward, people stood quietly in small groups.

Some approached him.

Some did not.

Mara remained beside him through both.

When the square began to empty, she placed her flowers beneath Ada’s name.

They were small yellow ones from the shop beside the library. She did not know what they were called.

Ada would have known.

Mara touched one petal.

Then she stood.

“Tea?” she asked Jonah.

He looked tired enough to be much older.

“Yes.”

At the station, Nell opened the door and went in ahead of them.

The bell rang.

For a moment Mara stopped on the threshold.

Then she followed.

---

Spring returned without making an announcement.

One Saturday there were daffodils near the platform wall. The next, someone arrived carrying rhubarb. Then the waiting room door could be left open, and people began complaining about the heat of a sun they had spent months requesting.

Mara changed the bus timetables.

Nell played a tune she had written herself and refused to give it a title.

Jonah repaired the baker’s scales.

Mrs. Bell started a list of people interested in another trip to the coast.

“This is a list,” Mara said. “Not a committee.”

“Of course.”

“And there will be no treasurer.”

“Certainly not.”

“Why have you written ‘treasurer’ next to the baker’s name?”

Mrs. Bell turned the page over.

One morning, a small brown bird flew into the waiting room.

It entered through the open door, crossed the room in a frantic line, and landed on the top edge of the timetable board.

Everyone froze.

“Don’t chase it,” Nell said at once.

“No one is chasing it,” Jonah said.

The bird turned its head.

Mara opened the second door, the one leading to the platform.

Cool air moved through the room.

They waited.

The bird shifted along the board.

Outside, somewhere in the hedge, another bird called.

It launched itself into the light.

This time it flew cleanly.

Nell watched it vanish.

“Was that—”

“No way to know,” Jonah said.

“I know.”

Mara stood by the open door.

The clock ticked behind her. Someone poured tea. The baker asked whether anyone had seen the plate of biscuits he had placed on the counter, and Mrs. Bell told him he was holding it.

Beyond the platform, the empty track bed curved toward the mountain.

For eleven years, Mara had watched that curve and waited for something to come back.

Now she stepped aside to let someone enter.

“Morning,” she said.

“Am I early?”

“No,” Mara told them. “Come in.”

---

The person at the door was carrying a large rolled map.

This was the first indication of trouble.

The second was the bright expression of someone who had already had an idea and was now looking for places to put it.

“Are you in charge?” the visitor asked.

“No,” Mara said.

From behind her, six people said, “Yes.”

She closed her eyes briefly.

“My name’s Felix Reed. I’m with the county paths project.”

“Tea?” Mrs. Bell asked.

“That would be lovely.”

This, Mara felt, was how institutions entered: not through force, but through an unguarded offer of tea.

Felix laid the map across the counter.

It showed the old railway line from Larkspur to the foot of the mountain. Parts were marked in green. Others were striped red.

“We’re exploring a walking route,” he said.

The waiting room grew quiet.

Mara looked at the map without touching it.

“Along the line?”

“Where access is safe and ownership can be agreed. Not through the tunnel, obviously. There’d be a diversion through the woods.”

Jonah came to stand beside her.

Felix noticed the change in the room.

“It’s only an initial proposal.”

“Who owns the cutting?” Jonah asked.

“Three different parties, which is one of the challenges.”

“And the culvert beyond the old signal?”

“That would need inspection.”

“It would.”

Felix drew a notebook from his pocket.

“Do you know the route?”

Jonah looked at Mara.

She was still studying the green line.

“Some of it,” he said.

Mrs. Bell put a cup beside the map, carefully avoiding the paper.

Mara traced the curve beyond the station with one finger.

She knew where the brambles were deepest. She knew where rain gathered and where foxes crossed at dusk. She knew the point at which the old line disappeared from the platform’s view.

She did not know what lay beyond that anymore.

“When would you walk it?” she asked.

Felix blinked.

“Well. We’re arranging a site visit next month.”

Mara looked at Jonah.

He nodded, barely.

“We’ll come,” she said.

---

The walk began badly.

Felix wore shoes that advertised confidence but not experience. The baker had come “for the fresh air” and brought enough sandwiches to undermine the expedition’s pace. Mrs. Bell carried a notebook despite repeated assurances that no minutes would be taken.

Nell had a stick.

Mara had sturdy boots she had bought three days earlier and already regretted breaking in so late.

Jonah carried a small bag of tools.

“Why?” Mara asked.

“Habit.”

“We’re walking.”

“Things break while people are walking.”

Ahead of them, Felix stopped at the edge of the cutting.

The station was only fifty yards behind, but the platform already looked different: smaller, with its windows reflecting the morning sky.

Mara turned once.

The door was locked. A notice gave their return time.

She had checked it twice.

Then she faced forward.

The old track bed was uneven under the grass. Here and there, half-buried sleepers remained, dark with rot. The rails had been taken, but not everything that had held them.

Nell stepped around a puddle.

“Imagine doing this in the dark.”

“No need,” Jonah said.

She glanced back at him, then lowered her stick.

They walked beneath branches just beginning to leaf.

After the first bend, the station disappeared.

Mara stopped.

No one spoke.

She could hear a stream she had forgotten.

It ran behind the bank, close enough that once she had known its sound from the ticket window on quiet days. After the line closed, the brambles had thickened and the sound had faded from her attention.

Now it was clear.

Water moving over stone.

Mara took another step.

---

They reached the old signal before noon.

Its post leaned slightly, paint flaking from the metal. The arm had been removed years earlier.

Jonah stood beneath it.

Felix opened his map.

“This is where the diversion would begin.”

Beyond the signal, the ground rose toward the tunnel approach.

A fence barred the way. Several warning signs had been fastened to it at different times, their wording growing more severe as their materials grew cheaper.

Mara looked through the wire.

The mouth of the tunnel was not visible from there.

She was grateful.

She was disappointed.

Both feelings sat together without resolving.

Jonah crouched beside the culvert.

Nell crouched too.

“Don’t put your hand in,” he said.

“I wasn’t.”

“You looked as if you might.”

“I have many expressions.”

He looked at her.

She grinned.

For the first time that morning, Mara smiled.

The baker announced lunch.

They sat on a dry bank facing the woods.

Mrs. Bell distributed napkins with the weary authority of a person whose planning had once again justified itself.

Felix asked Jonah about drainage.

Nell asked whether the route could have a bench.

“Several, we hope,” Felix said.

“One here.”

“Possibly.”

“Not possibly. People will be tired.”

The baker lifted his sandwich.

“An excellent point.”

Mara looked back along the way they had come.

From there, the cutting bent out of sight.

For so long, she had imagined the distance beyond the station as a single place: where the train had gone.

But it was not a single place.

It was a stream, a bank of primroses, a rusting signal, a culvert that needed clearing. It was somewhere to sit while a girl argued for a bench.

She ate her sandwich.

---

The route took two years.

There were surveys, permissions, objections, revisions, and one dispute over a strip of land so narrow the baker suggested they carry walkers across it.

Mara learned new forms of patience.

Jonah learned that engineers half his age could be both irritating and right.

Mrs. Bell became, despite all promises, secretary.

“I have not agreed to a committee,” Mara said.

“No,” Mrs. Bell replied. “You’re listed as an independent local adviser.”

“That sounds worse.”

“It gives you no responsibilities.”

“Then why am I holding this form?”

“Because you’re nearest the pen.”

Nell designed a small sign showing the birds visitors might see along the route. A naturalist corrected three of them, to her considerable annoyance.

The waiting room stayed open.

People came and went.

Some destinations were reached. Others changed.

The young man who had once asked about leaving town did leave, then sent a postcard, then returned for a weekend with a haircut no one understood.

The baby named Rose learned to walk. Her father brought her one Saturday, and she rang the bell so enthusiastically that Jonah had to check the fastening afterward.

Mara grew older without feeling that every year had been added to the same day.

She still woke at five.

Sometimes she got up.

Sometimes she did not.

On the second anniversary of Ada’s visit, she placed two barley sweets on the ticket counter and sat by the window until the clock passed six seventeen.

Nothing came.

She ate one sweet.

She took the other to Jonah.

---

When the path finally opened, the mayor arrived with oversized scissors.

Mara regarded them with distrust.

“Do they work?”

“They’re ceremonial,” Felix said.

“That wasn’t my question.”

The ribbon had been stretched across the platform gate. Children gathered at the front, impatient with all adult arrangements that stood between them and movement.

Nell, now nearly as tall as her mother, held a folded speech.

“I thought you weren’t speaking,” Mara said.

“I’m not.”

“What’s that?”

“Things I’ll say if someone makes me.”

“Good preparation.”

“Grandad says that. It’s very annoying.”

Jonah stood near the back with his hands in his coat pockets.

Mara went to him.

“Are you walking?”

“Some of it.”

“How much?”

“As much as I walk.”

She nodded.

The mayor began.

There were thanks, acknowledgments, and a phrase about journeys that made Mara stare firmly at the ground.

Then the scissors failed to cut the ribbon.

The children watched in delight.

Jonah sighed, took a small pair from his pocket, and stepped forward.

“Habit,” Mara murmured.

He handed them to the mayor.

The ribbon fell.

Everyone applauded.

Then, at last, they began.

---

Mara walked beside Jonah as far as the stream.

Behind them, voices rose and scattered among the trees.

Ahead, Nell was showing Rose how to distinguish a blackbird from a starling. Rose remained primarily interested in whether either could be persuaded to eat a piece of her bun.

At the old signal stood a bench.

Nell had won.

A small plaque read:

For anyone who needs a moment.

No names.

No instructions about how long.

Mara sat.

Jonah lowered himself beside her with a careful breath.

The woods were green and full of movement.

Somewhere beyond them lay the fenced approach to the tunnel. Somewhere behind them stood the station, with tea waiting and a clock that was occasionally slow.

Mara rested her hands on her knees.

“Was it enough?” she asked.

Jonah looked at her.

“What?”

She considered the question.

The waiting room. The path. The bell. The years since the morning Ada had stepped from the train.

She shook her head.

“I don’t know what I mean.”

He leaned back.

“Then we needn’t answer yet.”

A bird landed on the path, saw them, and moved into the grass.

Mara watched it go.

From beyond the bend came footsteps: someone approaching, unhurried.

She shifted along the bench to make room.

---

It was the baker, breathing harder than he intended to admit.

“Excellent placement,” he said, sitting.

“Nell’s idea,” Mara told him.

“Visionary child.”

He unwrapped a sandwich.

“You’ve only just had lunch,” Jonah said.

“This is the walk.”

“Not a separate meal.”

“Depends on the walk.”

They sat together while the crowd thinned.

Some people continued toward the woods. Some turned back. A little boy walked past counting his steps aloud, lost his number, and began again without concern.

At last the baker folded his paper.

“I’d better get back. Mrs. Bell’s near the urn, and she has views about tea strength.”

He stood.

“Coming?”

“In a minute,” Mara said.

He nodded and left them.

The stream ran beneath the old culvert, clear and quick.

Mara could hear it from the bench.

She thought she might be able to hear it from the station again if she opened the right window.

“I’ll try that,” she said.

Jonah turned.

“What?”

“The side window. The one behind the shelves.”

“What about it?”

“It used to open.”

“Probably still does.”

“It’s painted shut.”

He smiled.

“Most things are.”

She looked at him.

“Don’t.”

“I was only talking about windows.”

“Of course.”

He offered his arm when they stood.

She took it because the ground was uneven.

Then she kept it because she wished to.

---

The side window required two afternoons, a sharp blade, and a disagreement about whether the frame had swollen.

“It has,” Jonah said.

“It’s the paint.”

“It can be two things.”

Mara stood back.

This was a principle she was still learning.

When the window finally opened, it did so all at once, startling them both.

Cool air entered.

Dust shifted on the sill.

From beyond the platform came the sound of water.

Mara said nothing.

Jonah wiped the blade.

“There.”

She stood beside the opening.

The stream sounded smaller from inside.

Not less real.

Just farther away.

“That’s good,” she said.

He looked at her, then out through the window.

“Yes.”

They left it open.

By noon, three people had complained of a draft.

By one, someone had placed flowers on the sill.

By two, Mara had found a strip of wood to keep it from closing.

The waiting room went on.

---

Years later, when people tried to say when the place had changed, they disagreed.

Some said it was when the kettle arrived.

Some said it was the first concert.

Mrs. Bell maintained that adequate insurance had been the turning point, though nobody encouraged her.

Nell, who had gone away to study and returned whenever trains and buses permitted, said it was the morning a woman on a bus agreed to hold a box.

Mara refused to choose.

She had become suspicious of stories that made one moment responsible for everything.

Still, she kept the old return ticket.

It lay in the drawer beneath the counter, inside the folded timetable.

The paper was soft at the corners.

The crescent punch mark remained clear.

Once, Rose found it while looking for a pencil.

“Can I use this?”

“For what?”

“A bookmark.”

“No.”

“Why?”

“It belongs to someone.”

“Who?”

Mara took it gently.

“A man who came through.”

“Will he come back?”

She looked at the faded ink.

“I don’t know.”

Rose accepted this with the ease children sometimes have before adults teach them that every question requires comfort.

She found a pencil.

Mara put the ticket away.

Outside, footsteps crossed the platform.

The bell rang.

Someone called her name.

She closed the drawer and went to see who had arrived.The person at the door was not someone Mara knew.

That was becoming rarer.

She had grown accustomed to recognizing visitors by the way they entered. Mrs. Bell opened the door just far enough to announce herself before bringing in whatever she had decided the room required. The baker pushed it with one shoulder. Jonah lifted the latch slightly because he knew where it caught.

This visitor knocked.

Then, when no one immediately answered, she knocked again.

“You can come in,” Mara called.

The door opened a careful six inches.

“Are you Mara?”

“Yes.”

The woman stepped inside.

She wore a raincoat despite the clear weather and carried a narrow leather folder pressed against her chest. Her hair had been cut recently; a few short pieces still clung to the wool near her collar.

“I’m Ruth Vale.”

Mara’s hand remained on the drawer.

Outside, Rose was explaining to someone that the path had seventeen benches if you counted the low wall, which most people did not.

“Vale,” Mara said.

“My father was Elias Vale.”

The waiting room seemed to shift around the woman, not as it had on the morning of the train, but in the ordinary, difficult way a room changes when someone brings news.

“Was?”

Ruth nodded.

“Last winter.”

“I’m sorry.”

“Thank you.”

Mara looked at the folder.

“Would you like to sit down?”

“Yes. I think I would.”

She chose the bench beneath Ada’s photograph.

Mara put the kettle on.

There had been a time when she worried that this was an inadequate response to most human situations. Experience had persuaded her that inadequacy was not a good reason to withhold tea.

“Milk?”

“A little.”

“Sugar?”

“No.”

Ruth placed the folder on her knees.

“I wasn’t certain you’d still be here.”

“Neither was I, once.”

“My father talked about this station.”

“What did he say?”

“That there was someone he owed a ticket.”

Mara turned off the tap.

For a few seconds, water continued to fall from it.

“He left me a letter,” Ruth said. “Not to read. To deliver.”

“When?”

“In his will. There were instructions.”

She sounded embarrassed by the formality.

“I meant to come sooner. It’s been a complicated year.”

“You needn’t apologize to me.”

“No.”

Ruth watched Mara carry the cups.

“I’ve had a long time to get good at apologizing.”

Mara set one before her.

“Then you can practice something else while you’re here.”

Ruth smiled faintly.

From the folder she took an envelope.

Mara’s name was written on the front.

Not her surname. Not an address.

Only Mara, in a narrow, deliberate hand.

“Did you know him well?” Ruth asked.

“No.”

“But you knew him.”

“Yes.”

Ruth looked at the room.

The clock. The bell. The photographs and notices. A child’s mitten waiting on the windowsill for someone to remember it.

“He said you would.”

Mara took the envelope.

It was unsealed.

Inside were two sheets of paper and a photograph of a man standing beside the sea.

Elias was older in the picture than she remembered him. His shoulders had thickened; his hair had gone white. He was smiling at someone outside the frame.

Mara studied the date printed on the back.

Then she read the letter.

Dear Mara,

If Ruth has found you, I have probably caused her some inconvenience. Please offer her tea. She will say she cannot stay if she thinks she is troubling you.

Mara glanced at Ruth.

“I’ve got time,” Ruth said quickly.

Mara looked down again.

I have tried several times to write an account of what happened. Each attempt became less accurate as it became more complete.

You sold me a ticket. I used it. I came home.

Those are the parts I can state without qualifications.

I do not know why I was allowed to return in the order I did. I do not know whether “allowed” is the right word. I have no explanation you could carry to someone else and expect them to understand.

For years I thought an explanation was what I owed you.

Now I think I may owe you something simpler.

I lived.

I went back to the house. I met my daughter. I learned that grief does not become tidy because it is interrupted by happiness. There were arguments. There were bills. The roof needed replacing twice, which I still consider unreasonable.

I was not always grateful in a way that would make a satisfying story.

But I lived.

I hope you did too.

Mara lowered the page.

Ruth had turned her cup between her hands until the handle pointed away from her.

“Are you all right?” she asked.

“Yes.”

Mara looked again at the photograph.

“Were you taking this?”

“Yes. At Whitcombe.”

“I’ve been there.”

“Then you know about the wind.”

“It stole a napkin.”

“It took my hat.”

Ruth’s voice loosened slightly.

“Dad went after it. We told him not to. He was nearly eighty.”

“Did he get it?”

“No. He got someone else’s.”

Mara laughed.

Ruth did too, then pressed the side of one finger beneath her nose.

There was one more paragraph.

There is a box in my old workshop. Ruth knows which one. I have asked her to bring it if she wishes, but not to let me make a duty of everything simply because I am dead.

You are under no obligation to accept it.

If you do, please do not keep it shut out of respect.

Elias

Mara folded the letter.

“What’s in the box?”

Ruth looked toward the door.

“It’s in the car.”

“That wasn’t an answer.”

“No,” Ruth said. “It wasn’t.”

---

The box was longer than Mara expected.

They carried it between them from a small grey car parked beside the library. Ruth took the heavier end without discussion.

By the time they reached the station, the baker had spotted them.

“What have you got?”

“A box,” Mara said.

He walked alongside.

“Promising shape.”

“For what?”

“Something interesting.”

“Then we must try not to disappoint you.”

He held the door.

They set the box on the counter.

Jonah arrived five minutes later, because news in Larkspur moved according to rules no one had successfully measured.

He looked at Ruth, then the box, then Mara.

“Should I ask?”

“Not yet.”

“Tools?”

“Yes.”

He nodded, which was one of the many forms of conversation they had developed.

Ruth removed the lid herself.

Inside, packed in yellowing newspaper, was a model railway station.

Not a train.

A station.

Its roof lifted off in two sections. There were benches, a ticket window, and tiny doors with brass hinges no bigger than the edge of a fingernail. A clock stood above the platform.

Mara leaned closer.

It was Larkspur.

Not as it looked now.

There was no path and no flower-filled windowsill. The old timetable board hung beside the entrance. A painted poster advertised a summer excursion whose lettering Mara remembered from a photograph.

At the counter sat a tiny figure in a blue dress.

Mara touched her own sleeve.

“Did he make this?”

Ruth nodded.

“For years. He used to work on it after supper.”

Jonah bent over the model.

“That guttering,” he murmured.

The baker looked at the minute ticket window.

“I feel taller already.”

Mara did not answer.

At the edge of the platform stood a second figure with a brown suitcase.

Beside him, empty rails curved toward the corner of the wooden base.

“Why did he make it?” she asked.

“I don’t know.”

Ruth took a small packet from the box.

“These go with it.”

Inside were more figures.

A woman holding a newspaper above a child.

A boy beside a wooden horse.

A conductor with one arm raised.

Mara sat down.

Jonah moved the cup near her before it could be knocked over.

Ruth began to put the packet away.

“I shouldn’t have—”

“No.”

Mara reached for it.

“Let me see.”

One by one, she laid the figures on the counter.

The paint was not quite precise enough to give them faces. A touch of color suggested a mouth. A darker stroke marked hair beneath a hat.

But the postures were exact.

Waiting had a shape.

She knew each one.

At the bottom of the packet was a woman carrying an overnight bag.

One arm had been lifted slightly, as though the maker had considered a wave and then stopped.

Mara picked her up.

“She should be by the third window,” she said.

Ruth looked at her.

Mara stood.

Together, they removed the roof, arranged the platform, and placed the figures where Elias had intended them to be.

The baker grew quiet.

When they were finished, Jonah checked the underside of the base.

“There’s a compartment.”

Ruth frowned.

“I’ve never seen that.”

He pressed a small wooden catch.

A drawer slid open.

Inside lay a tiny yellow sign.

Mara read it aloud.

“Caution. Wet floor.”

For a moment nobody spoke.

Then she laughed so hard she had to sit down again.

---

The model did not immediately go on display.

This disappointed several people, including the baker, who had already imagined showing it to visitors with the authority of a man who had carried none of it.

Mara put the lid back on the box.

“I need to think.”

“Of course,” Ruth said.

She sounded relieved.

They ate lunch at the café near the library, because the waiting room had accumulated many things but not a reliable means of producing lunch.

Ruth ordered soup.

Mara ordered the same.

For a while they spoke about Elias.

He had liked making things he could hold in one hand. He had hated buying clothes. He had planted a pear tree that never produced a pear and defended it for twenty-three years.

“He said it was thinking,” Ruth told her.

“Perhaps it was.”

“It finally fruited the year after he died.”

“How many?”

“Two.”

“A restrained tribute.”

Ruth smiled into her spoon.

Then she said, “I’m not sure what I’m supposed to do with everything.”

Mara waited.

“The house, I mean. His tools. All the things he kept because they might be useful.”

“Are they?”

“Some of them. To someone.”

“That makes it difficult.”

Ruth looked up sharply.

“Everyone says I should be practical.”

“People are often generous with advice about objects they don’t have to touch.”

There was a chair in Elias’s kitchen, Ruth said, with a split in the back that he had repaired using a curved piece of brass. It was ugly. It did not match the other chairs. She could not bear to get rid of it.

“Then don’t.”

“I can’t keep everything.”

“No. But you can keep a chair.”

Ruth stared at her bowl.

“I thought coming here would be one more thing to finish.”

“Has it been?”

“No.”

Outside the café, a delivery driver was trying to maneuver a stack of crates through a doorway too narrow to reward optimism.

Mara watched him turn them sideways.

“Would you like to walk?” she asked.

Ruth looked toward the station.

“Along the old line?”

“As far as you like.”

---

They stopped at the bench beneath the signal.

Ruth read the plaque.

“For anyone who needs a moment.”

“My friend’s granddaughter insisted on the bench,” Mara said.

“Was it difficult to get?”

“Less difficult than getting her to accept that the plaque didn’t need a drawing of the bird.”

“What bird?”

“All of them.”

Ruth sat.

She had taken off her raincoat. Beneath it she wore a cardigan with one cuff coming unstitched.

Mara noticed the loose thread and resisted the urge to mention it.

“I don’t remember him coming home,” Ruth said.

Mara sat beside her.

“My father. I was too young.”

“How young?”

“Not quite three.”

The stream moved beneath them.

“I remember other people remembering it. My mother telling the story. Neighbors stopping in the street. I used to think I remembered the door opening, but now I think I only remember being told.”

Mara looked down at her hands.

“What did your mother say?”

“That he looked as if he’d been walking in the rain.”

“Had he?”

“He wouldn’t answer questions properly. Not then.”

Ruth gave a small, tired shrug.

“He said he got lost.”

Mara thought of his clean shoes, the broken clasp on the suitcase.

“And afterward?”

“He said the same thing with more words.”

A cyclist passed slowly and thanked them for moving their feet.

Ruth waited until he was gone.

“I thought he was hiding something.”

“Perhaps he was.”

“Do you know what?”

Mara looked toward the woods.

There were things she could say.

A train had come to a line without rails. Her sister had stepped down onto a platform after eleven years. A man had boarded with a return ticket.

Each statement was both true and unlikely to be useful.

“I know he wanted to get home,” she said.

Ruth rubbed the frayed cuff between her fingers.

“He was there,” she said. “Afterward. He was there for everything.”

Mara nodded.

“Even when I didn’t want him to be.”

“That tends to happen.”

“I should have asked him more.”

“Perhaps.”

Ruth turned toward her.

Mara let the word remain.

There was no kindness in telling someone that every unfinished conversation could be dismissed. Some things were regrettable. They could be regretted without becoming the whole of a life.

After a while Ruth said, “I did ask him a lot.”

“I believe you.”

“He was very good at talking about the pear tree instead.”

“That sounds like a man with a strategy.”

Ruth laughed.

They sat until the light began to change.

Then Mara asked whether she had somewhere to stay.

“I thought I’d drive home.”

“How far?”

“Three hours.”

“It’s already four.”

“I drive at night.”

“So do many people. It isn’t a qualification.”

Ruth looked at her.

Mara looked back.

There was a room above the café, as it happened.

They walked back to inquire.

---

That evening, Mara telephoned Nell.

“I need advice.”

“From me?”

“Don’t sound so alarmed.”

“I’m only trying to appreciate the occasion.”

Nell was living in a city where buses ran all night and she still complained there were never any when required. Through the telephone Mara could hear music, traffic, and someone asking where a saucepan had gone.

“There’s a model of the station,” Mara said.

“A model?”

“Elias made it.”

There was a brief silence.

Nell knew enough of the story to understand which Elias.

“What’s it like?”

“Beautiful. Unsettling.”

“Those aren’t opposites.”

“I know.”

“Do you want it?”

Mara sat at the kitchen table.

“I think I do.”

“Then what’s the question?”

“Whether I want other people looking at it.”

Nell said nothing.

Mara could hear a door close at the other end.

At last Nell said, “You don’t have to put it in the waiting room.”

“I know.”

“You could put it in your house.”

“Yes.”

“Or leave it in the box for a while.”

“He said not to.”

“Was that advice or a haunting?”

Mara laughed.

“Advice, I think.”

“Then he can tolerate disagreement.”

After the call, Mara returned to the station.

She turned on only the light above the counter.

The box waited where they had left it.

She lifted the lid.

In the model, every window was dark.

For some reason this troubled her more than the figures.

She stood looking at the tiny waiting room, then reached in and removed the roof.

It came away easily.

There was a wire beneath the platform.

At one end, hidden behind the base, was a small switch.

Mara pressed it.

Warm light filled the windows.

“Oh,” she said.

She stood there for a long time.

---

Ruth stayed two nights.

On the second afternoon, she and Jonah carried the model to Mara’s house.

It went on the table in the room she still called the spare room, though no one had slept there since Nell had stayed for a concert years earlier.

Mara cleared a shelf.

Jonah made a stand.

Ruth packed the loose newspaper.

No one attempted to make the event more important than it was.

When they finished, Mara plugged in the little lights.

The station glowed.

Ruth sat on the edge of the bed.

“He’d have liked that.”

Mara looked at her.

“I think so too.”

The following morning, Ruth brought her bag down from the café and came to say goodbye.

Mara gave her the photograph of Elias by the sea.

“I think this should stay with you.”

Ruth held it between both hands.

“There’s another copy.”

“Then we can both have one.”

Ruth nodded.

At the door, she hesitated.

“I’ve been thinking about the chair.”

“The one with the brass?”

“Yes.”

“Have you decided?”

“I’m keeping it.”

“Good.”

“And I’ve booked someone to look at the roof.”
