Ada smiled.

“I’m writing that down.”

“Please don’t.”

But she did.

---

The sentence appeared six months later in a funding application.

Rose was furious.

Imogen was delighted.

The application succeeded.

The waiting room acquired new insulation, an accessible entrance, and enough money to stop pretending the kettle’s noise was normal.

Rose conceded that the quotation had been useful.

She did not concede that this justified it.

Ada brought her a mug printed with the words.

Rose refused to use it.

Leah used it every Saturday.

---

In late autumn, a storm brought down a beech tree across the path.

The route closed.

People gathered at the barrier as if the tree had committed a breach of manners.

Rose went with the maintenance team.

The trunk was enormous.

Inside the split wood, the grain shone pale and fresh.

She placed a hand against it.

The tree had been standing when Mara took her first walk.

Perhaps before Mara was born.

Now it lay across the way, requiring saws, judgment, and several people who knew what they were doing.

They cleared the path.

Not all the wood was removed.

A section remained beside the stream.

By spring, moss had begun to soften its surface.

Children climbed on it.

Birds landed there.

The fallen tree became part of the route.

Not a lesson.

Not a symbol anyone needed to agree on.

A tree.

---

Leah made a tiny version for the model.

Ada protested.

“It makes the path look blocked.”

“It was blocked.”

“But it isn’t now.”

Leah considered.

Then she cut the miniature trunk into sections and placed them beside the path.

“There.”

Ada looked.

“That works.”

Rose watched them through the cabinet glass.

The little world had weather.

History.

Repairs.

Arguments over accuracy.

It was becoming suspiciously like the large one.

---

One morning, Rose arrived to find a small paper bag hanging from the station door.

Inside were barley sweets.

No note.

She stood holding them.

The square was almost empty.

A bakery van turned the corner.

Someone opened a window above the café.

Rose looked toward the platform.

The track bed lay quiet beneath frost.

No rails.

No train.

She took out her phone and sent a photograph to Nell.

A reply came a minute later.

Ada bought them yesterday. Forgot to leave a note.

Rose laughed.

Then another message arrived.

Sorry if she gave you a moment.

Rose looked at the sweets.

She typed:

A good one.

Then she unlocked the door.

---

She put the sweets in a dish beside the counter.

By noon, half were gone.

A child declared them too hard.

An elderly woman said that was the point.

Leah took one and spent ten minutes unable to answer questions clearly.

Rose regarded this as an improvement.

At closing, one sweet remained.

She wrapped it again and put it in her pocket.

On the walk home, she stopped at the library shelter.

A bus was approaching.

She had no reason to take it.

Not that day.

She watched it slow.

The doors opened.

The driver looked at her.

“Getting on?”

Rose considered.

Then she smiled.

“Yes,” she said.

She paid for a ticket.

The printed paper was light in her hand.

She sat by the window as the square began to move.

At the station, the windows reflected the afternoon sky.

Then the bus turned, and it was gone from view.

---

At the third stop, a child boarded with a shoebox.

Rose looked up.

The child sat across the aisle.

A woman beside him carried a shopping bag and the expression of someone whose day had already contained more negotiations than expected.

The shoebox moved.

Rose closed her eyes briefly.

Then she opened them.

“What’s in the box?”

The child looked at her.

“A frog.”

“Alive?”

“Obviously.”

The woman sighed.

“Not obviously to everyone, Toby.”

“It’s going to the pond.”

“From where?”

“Our bath.”

Rose looked at the woman.

“There’s an explanation,” she said.

“I’m sure.”

The child lifted the lid an inch.

A small green head appeared.

The woman pushed it down gently.

“Not on the bus.”

Rose turned toward the window and began to laugh.

Not loudly.

Not at them.

At the world’s refusal to repeat itself exactly while continuing to use some of the same materials.

---

The pond was two stops beyond where Rose had intended to get off, although she had not intended very firmly.

She went with them.

The frog’s release required less waiting than the bird’s had.

Toby opened the box.

The frog leaped.

Everyone made a noise.

Then it was in the reeds.

The child crouched by the water.

“Will it remember us?”

His mother looked at Rose.

Rose considered the question.

“I don’t know.”

Toby nodded.

“Will it be okay?”

“I hope so.”

He watched the reeds.

Then stood.

“Can we get chips?”

His mother laughed with visible relief.

“Yes.”

They invited Rose.

She accepted.

---

When Rose returned to Larkspur, the sky was turning pink above the library.

She walked past the station.

The waiting room was closed.

Through the window, she could see the model cabinet.

For a moment, she thought the little lights were on.

Then she realized it was the sunset caught in the glass.

She stood anyway.

In her pocket, the last barley sweet had warmed in her hand.

She unwrapped it.

Across the square, the bell above the café door rang as someone came out.

The sound was thinner than the station bell.

Ordinary.

Enough.

Rose went home to write about the frog.

---

Her first sentence was:

There was an explanation.

She looked at it.

Then she added:

There usually is, though not always the one you want.

She stopped.

Too wise.

She crossed out the second sentence.

Then she wrote:

The frog had been in the bath for six hours.

That was better.

---

In the waiting room the following Saturday, Leah read the page.

She laughed.

“So this is what you did on Tuesday.”

“Yes.”

“You could have told us.”

“I’m telling you now.”

“With revisions.”

“Everything has revisions.”

Leah looked up.

Rose pointed at the notebook.

“Don’t.”

Leah smiled and turned the page.

Outside, Toby and his mother crossed the square.

They had found the waiting room.

Toby carried nothing.

Rose considered this promising.

He came in and looked around.

“Where’s the train?”

Leah and Rose exchanged a glance.

Then Rose bent slightly.

“There isn’t one.”

“Why is there a station?”

“That,” Rose said, “is a longer story.”

Toby thought about it.

“Do you have time?”

The kettle had just boiled.

Rose pulled out a chair.

“Yes,” she said. “I think we do.” 
---

She did not begin with the train.

She began with a woman who sold tickets.

Toby interrupted after the first sentence.

“Did she like trains?”

Rose considered.

“She liked knowing where they were going.”

“Is that the same?”

“No.”

“Okay.”

He settled into the chair.

His mother accepted tea.

Leah took the small sign from the doorway because the floor was dry now.

Rose told him about the station closing.

About Mara keeping it clean.

About the yellow sign.

Toby looked at the sign in Leah’s hand.

“That one?”

“Not exactly.”

“Where’s the old one?”

“I don’t know.”

He frowned.

“Did it break?”

“Probably.”

“Can signs break?”

“Yes.”

He thought about this.

Then he said, “Go on.”

Rose went on.

---

When she reached the train, Toby leaned forward.

His mother looked at Rose.

Rose kept her voice ordinary.

The rails returned.

The whistle sounded.

The train stopped.

Toby asked whether it was a steam engine.

Rose showed him the number written in Mara’s notebook.

He nodded as though this settled a question of considerable importance.

Then Ada stepped onto the platform.

Rose paused.

Toby watched her.

“Was she a ghost?”

“I don’t know.”

“Did Mara know?”

“I don’t think she asked.”

“Why?”

Rose looked at the photograph on the wall.

“Perhaps there was something more important to say.”

Toby considered.

“What?”

Rose told him.

---

The room had grown quieter.

Not silent.

There were still cups.

Still a page turning.

Still someone outside trying to start a bicycle with an electric motor that did not appreciate the weather.

Rose did not tell the story as if the waiting room had ceased to exist around it.

It was part of the story.

She spoke of the bus.

Nell.

The bird.

Jonah.

Toby frowned at the idea that someone could be responsible and not responsible at the same time.

Rose did not simplify it.

His mother asked a question.

Leah answered.

The story moved around the table rather than remaining entirely in Rose’s hands.

This was good.

It had been in one person’s hands long enough.

---

At the end, Toby looked disappointed.

“What?” Rose asked.

“The train didn’t come back?”

“No.”

“Not even once?”

“Not that we know.”

He looked toward the platform.

Then at the people in the room.

“What about all this?”

Rose waited.

He waved a hand.

“The tea. The model. The frog.”

His mother smiled.

“The frog wasn’t in the story.”

“It was for me.”

Rose sat back.

“Yes,” she said. “I suppose it was.”

Toby seemed satisfied.

He went to inspect the model.

---

His mother remained at the table.

“Thank you,” she said.

“For what?”

“The story.”

“You’re welcome.”

“Is it true?”

Rose looked at her.

“I believe Mara.”

The woman nodded slowly.

Then she glanced toward Toby, who was counting the miniature windows.

“He’ll ask me about it for weeks.”

“I’m sorry.”

“No,” she said. “I think that’s all right.”

They drank the last of the tea.

---

After they left, Leah helped Rose put the chairs back.

“You told it differently.”

“From the notebook?”

“Yes.”

“I probably did.”

“You left things out.”

“I couldn’t tell all of it.”

“You added the frog.”

“Toby did.”

Leah smiled.

The model lights glowed.

Rose stood before them.

There were so many small figures now.

Passengers.

Walkers.

A child beside the stream.

A man with tools.

Three Maras.

She had once worried that adding to the scene would make it less true.

Now she was no longer certain what less true meant in a place that had continued to live.

She switched off the lights.

Then the room’s lights.

Then she opened the door.

Cold air entered.

The bell rang once above her head.

---

Across the square, the library had closed.

The bus shelter held one person reading a message.

The bakery windows were dark.

Rose walked slowly.

She thought of Mara’s first morning away.

The blue bag.

The light ticket.

The world rearranging itself.

Then she thought of the frog.

It had not known the story it entered.

Neither had Nell.

Neither had Jonah.

Neither had she.

At the corner, she paused to let a cyclist pass.

The cyclist thanked her.

Rose continued.

---

At home, she found a message from Amina.

Her mother had made a full recovery.

They were planning a visit in spring.

Did the waiting room have space for eight?

Rose wrote:

We can move the chairs.

Then she put the phone down and made supper.

There were tomatoes on the counter.

She cut one.

Red all the way through.

She ate a slice with salt before adding the rest to the pan.

The kitchen window reflected her face.

Behind it, the room was warm.

---

Later, she opened the green notebook.

She wrote:

Toby asked whether all this counted as the train coming back.

She stopped.

Outside, a car passed.

The refrigerator clicked.

She wrote:

I told him no.

Then:

I may have answered too quickly.

She looked at the page for a while.

Then she closed the notebook, leaving the question where it was.

---

The following spring, eight people arrived with food.

Amina had misunderstood the question about space as a question about lunch.

The waiting room accommodated this.

Tables moved.

Chairs appeared.

Someone fetched plates from home.

Her mother sat by the side window and listened to the stream.

Leah arrived late and was hugged before she could remove her coat.

“You drove so far,” the older woman said.

Leah looked embarrassed.

“It was only a drive.”

“No.”

The woman held her hands.

“No.”

Leah stopped trying to make it smaller.

Rose watched.

That, too, took practice.

---

They ate together.

There were dishes Rose could not name and immediately wanted to eat again.

The baker’s successor contributed bread.

Imogen found more cups.

Ada played a little music.

Toby arrived halfway through with his mother and was absorbed into the meal with no formal decision.

He asked Amina’s mother whether she had been on the special train.

“No,” she said.

“Okay.”

Then he asked whether she liked frogs.

The conversation improved from there.

---

After lunch, they walked as far as the signal.

Not everyone went.

Some stayed behind.

Amina’s mother sat on the bench with Ruth, who had come by arrangement and immediately acquired a plate to take home.

Two women who had never met spoke about operations, grandchildren, difficult fruit trees, and the merits of chairs with arms.

Rose stood a little way off.

Nell came beside her.

“What are you thinking?”

“That we need another bench.”

Nell laughed.

“Practical.”

“Usually.”

They looked at the path.

People were walking in both directions.

There was room.

Not always enough.

Enough to begin with.

---

The new bench was funded by a small collection.

No plaque.

No dedication.

Just a bench near the first.

Rose helped install it.

Leah checked the fixings.

Ada sat down to test it.

“Comfortable?”

“Very.”

“Then move. We need to finish tightening it.”

Ada did not move.

Leah sighed.

“Independent local adviser,” she said.

Rose looked at her.

Leah smiled.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

They finished the bench.

By the end of the afternoon, someone else was using it.

Success did not always require a ceremony.

---

In the model, there was no room for a second bench.

Leah tried three arrangements.

None worked.

At last she removed a small bush.

Ada objected.

“That bird lives there.”

“It’s painted.”

“It still lives there.”

Leah looked at Rose.

Rose raised both hands.

“Not my department.”

They moved the bird to a tree.

The bench fit.

The tiny world continued negotiating.

---

Outside, a real bird landed on the platform railing.

It was brown.

Small.

Entirely unremarkable to anyone who did not know how much work it took to remain alive through winter.

Rose watched it.

The bird watched something else.

Then it flew.

Inside, the kettle boiled.

Rose turned toward the door.

There was no need to decide whether the bird meant anything before making tea.

---

The summer that followed was dry.

The stream narrowed.

The path dusted people’s shoes.

The waiting room door remained open until closing, and sometimes a little longer.

On hot afternoons, people sat without talking.

Rose learned that silence could be crowded without being uncomfortable.

One day, a man slept on the bench for two hours.

Imogen asked whether they should wake him.

“Is he all right?”

“I think so.”

“Then not yet.”

He woke embarrassed.

They gave him water.

He had been working nights.

The bus he needed was in forty minutes.

He stayed.

The waiting room did not ask him to justify the sleep.

---

At the end of the summer, Leah announced she was moving.

Not far enough to be tragic.

Far enough to alter Saturdays.

Rose said she was pleased.

She was.

She was also not.

Leah understood.

“I’ll come back.”

“I know.”

“I can take the model repairs home.”

“We’ll manage.”

“I know.”

They stood beside the cabinet.

The little station shone.

For a moment Rose wanted to tell Leah not to go.

Instead she asked about the new workshop.

Leah’s face changed.

She began describing the windows.

Rose listened.

---

On Leah’s last regular Saturday, they gave her a key.

She looked at it.

“I already have one.”

“This is to keep.”

She turned it over.

Rose had threaded it onto a plain ring.

No inscription.

No date.

Leah closed her hand around it.

“Thank you.”

“You may have to let yourself in.”

“I will.”

They hugged.

Later, Rose watched her car leave.

Then she went inside, where a woman was waiting to ask whether the buses ran on bank holidays.

Some did.

Some did not.

They worked it out.

---

Autumn brought rain.

The roof held.

Rose found this satisfying every time.

She stood beneath the dry ceiling and listened to water strike the slates.

The station had once been built to move people elsewhere.

Now it held them while they decided.

Neither purpose was small.

She wrote that down.

Then, after some thought, left it uncrossed.

---

One November evening, the lights failed across the square.

The waiting room went dark.

Someone swore.

Someone laughed.

Ada found the emergency lamp.

Imogen checked the fuse box and confirmed it was not theirs.

Rose opened the cupboard for candles, then remembered they were not permitted to use them.

“Insurance,” she said.

Mrs. Bell would have been pleased.

They set the emergency lamp on the table.

Its light was ugly and practical.

The model remained dark.

People moved closer together.

Without the hum of the refrigerator, the room sounded older.

The clock ticked.

The stream ran.

Someone began telling a story.

Not Mara’s.

A story about a train missed in another country, a wedding, and a goat.

By the time the lights returned, everyone objected to the brightness.

Rose turned half of them off.

They listened to the end.

---

The goat, it turned out, had not attended the wedding.

This disappointed Toby.

He was older now but retained standards.

The storyteller apologized.

“You can’t put a goat in the beginning and not bring it back.”

Rose looked at him.

He had no idea what he had said.

She smiled.

“Sometimes you can.”

He considered arguing.

Then decided the story was over enough.

They made more tea.

---

At closing, the clock was five minutes slow.

Rose set it.

She had learned to do this without making a private occasion of it every time.

The mechanism was a mechanism.

Memory did not require all objects to remain sacred.

Still, she touched the wooden case before closing the glass.

A small habit.

Her own.

Then she put away the chair.

---

The next morning, she received a photograph from Leah.

A new workbench.

A large window.

On the sill stood a tiny yellow sign.

CAUTION. WET FLOOR.

Rose laughed.

She sent back:

Does it fold?

Leah replied:

Of course.

Rose put the phone down.

Outside, rain moved against the kitchen window.

There would be wet floors at the waiting room.

She went to find her coat.

---

At the station, Imogen was already there.

“You’re early.”

“So are you.”

“I had letters.”

“I had rain.”

They looked at each other.

Then smiled.

The kettle began its low rising sound.

Rose opened the side window a little.

Cold air entered.

The stream was loud after the night’s rain.

She listened.

Then she turned to the first envelope.

---

The letter was from a school.

They wanted to bring a class to hear about the railway.

Rose looked at the proposed date.

A Wednesday.

She wrote back.

Yes.

There is no train.

Please bring coats.

Then she paused and added:

There will be time for questions.

She knew this was a considerable promise.

She made it anyway.

---

The children came in pairs, attached to one another by instructions rather than string.

They filled the room.

The model became the center of gravity.

Rose spoke about the railway.

The goods it carried.

The people.

The tunnel.

She did not begin with the strange part.

A child asked about it anyway.

“My grandad says a train came back.”

Rose looked at the teacher.

The teacher looked interested.

“Yes,” Rose said. “There’s a story about that.”

“Is it true?”

“I believe the woman who told it.”

Another child raised a hand.

“Is that different?”

Rose smiled.

“Yes.”

The teacher took out a notebook.

Rose sighed inwardly.

There it was again.

---

They asked questions for forty minutes.

Some were about history.

Some were about ghosts.

One was about whether railway workers were allowed to keep abandoned sandwiches.

Rose answered what she could.

At the end, a quiet child approached the counter.

“My dad died,” she said.

The room seemed to move farther away.

Rose knelt.

“I’m sorry.”

“Could he come on the train?”

Rose looked at her.

The child waited.

There were answers that would be easy for Rose and cruel to the child.

She would not use them.

“I don’t know,” she said.

The child’s mouth trembled.

Rose stayed where she was.

“I wish I could tell you yes.”

“Did you wait for someone?”

“Yes.”

“Did they come?”

“No.”

The child looked down.

Rose added, “But I still have people to talk to about him.”

The teacher came closer, slowly.

The child leaned against her.

Rose did not turn the moment into a lesson.

She only stayed until the girl was ready to go.

---

After the class left, Rose sat on the bench.

The room was untidy.

Small damp footprints crossed the floor.

A pencil lay beneath the chair.

Imogen came with tea.

Rose took it.

For a while, neither spoke.

Then Rose said, “I don’t want the story to become a promise.”

“It doesn’t have to.”

“People will hear one.”

“Some will.”

Rose looked at the photograph of Ada.

Imogen sat beside her.

“We can tell it carefully.”

Rose nodded.

Carefully was not perfectly.

It would have to do.

---

That evening, she added a page to the green notebook.

She wrote about the child.

Not her name.

Not the school.

Only the question.

Then she wrote:

A story can open a door it cannot guarantee anyone will come through.

She sat with that sentence.

It was not too wise.

It was simply something she needed to remember.

She left it.

---

In spring, a letter arrived from the teacher.

The child was doing better.

Not finished.

Not cured.

Better.

They had begun a memory box at home.

The teacher thanked Rose for answering honestly.

Rose folded the letter.

She put it beside Mara’s notebook.

Not inside it.

Beside it.

There was room for more than one account.

---

The waiting room continued changing.

The library took responsibility for the book shelves.

A local repair group used the tables on Thursday evenings.

The bus company finally corrected the station on its digital map.

Tourists stopped arriving in quite such a state of surprise.

Some still did.

Maps could only do so much.

The clock remained.

The bell remained.

The side window swelled every damp winter.

Rose learned to free it with a blade and patience.

She never did decide whether the problem was the paint or the wood.

It could be two things.

---

On the anniversary of Mara’s bus ride, Rose traveled to Marsh End.

She did not do this every year.

That would have made another duty.

She went because the day was clear and she wanted to.

The bus passed the supermarket, the plant nursery, the field with houses.

More houses now.

The world continued accepting permission no one remembered giving.

Rose sat by the window.

A boy across the aisle held a violin case.

No box.

She was almost disappointed.

Then he opened a book and ignored her.

An entirely reasonable beginning.

---

At Marsh End, Jonah’s old house had a blue door.

The rosebush was gone.

A row of herbs grew beneath the kitchen window.

Rose stood at the lane for a moment, then walked on.

She did not knock.

The house did not owe her access.

At the end of the village, she found a bench overlooking fields.

She sat.

A tractor moved slowly near the far hedge.

Somewhere, a dog barked with committed displeasure.

Rose took out a barley sweet.

She had bought a packet at the shop.

It was not the same sweet.

It did not need to be.

---

On the return bus, she wrote in the green notebook.

I went to see a house and did not go in.

She considered adding an explanation.

Then she left the sentence alone.

The bus turned onto the river road.

Sunlight flashed on the water.

Rose closed the notebook and watched.

---

When she reached Larkspur, the waiting room was open.

Through the window, she saw someone sitting in Mara’s chair.

A young woman.

Head bent.

Phone charging by her feet.

Rose entered.

The bell rang.

The woman looked up.

“Sorry. Is this seat taken?”

Rose smiled.

“No.”

Imogen handed her a cup.

“How was the trip?”

“Good.”

“Find what you were looking for?”

Rose looked at the young woman, the model, the open side window.

“I think so.”

Then she sat in another chair.

---

Outside, the afternoon moved across the square.

A bus arrived.

A bus left.

The library clock was three minutes ahead of the station clock.

The café clock was wrong in a direction no one had established.

People managed.

They missed things.

They caught things.

They arrived too early and waited.

They arrived too late and made other arrangements.

The waiting room held a small part of all this.

Not enough to repair the world.

Enough to alter a journey.

Rose drank her tea.

Someone opened the door.

---

It was a man carrying a folded walking frame.

He set it down carefully.

“Would you have room for this?”

“For you to use?”

“For someone. It was my wife’s.”

Rose looked at the frame.

Then at him.

“Yes,” she said. “We can find room.”

He nodded too quickly.

“It’s in good condition.”

“I can see.”

“She only used it a few weeks.”

Rose pulled out a chair.

“Would you like to sit?”

He looked at the chair.

Then the door.

Then the frame.

“Yes,” he said.

Rose put on the kettle.

---

His wife’s name had been Margaret.

She disliked the frame because it made a noise on the kitchen tiles.

He had put felt pads on it.

Then rubber.

Then nothing, because neither solution worked properly and she accused him of making a second career of it.

He smiled when he said this.

Then he cried.

Rose sat.

The kettle boiled and switched off.

No one hurried her to pour.

Across the room, Imogen quietly moved a conversation toward the platform.

The man took out a handkerchief.

“I came to get rid of it.”

Rose looked at the frame.

“Would you rather take it home?”

He thought.

“No.”

“Then we’ll keep it.”

He nodded.

The tea had cooled slightly.

Rose poured it anyway.

---

Months later, the frame helped another woman cross the square after an operation.

She returned it with flowers tied to the handle.

Rose took a photograph and sent it to the man.

He came in the following week.

He stood looking at the frame.

Then he said, “She’d have liked the flowers.”

Rose nodded.

They left the ribbon on.

---

In the model, Leah could not make a walking frame small enough to satisfy herself.

She tried.

The first looked like a fence.

The second like an unusually determined insect.

The third worked.

Rose placed it near the waiting room bench.

Ada looked at it.

“Who’s it for?”

“Anyone who needs it.”

Ada nodded.

The miniature room had become crowded.

They moved a chair.

There was room.

---

One summer evening, Rose sat alone beneath the clock and read Mara’s notebook from beginning to end.

It took less time than she expected.

The events inside it had occupied most of Rose’s life.

On paper, they occupied a few hours.

She reached the final line.

The bus stop is outside the library.

She closed the cover.

For a while, she listened to the room.

The clock.

The stream.

A late bus in the square.

Then she took out the green notebook.

There were still blank pages.

She turned to one and wrote the date.

Outside, footsteps approached the door.

Rose set down the pen.

The latch lifted.

---

The person entering was old enough to remember the railway running.

He had not been to Larkspur in decades.

His name was Daniel.

He stood in the doorway looking at the clock.

“My mother used to tell me it was slow.”

“It still is, occasionally.”

He smiled.

“That’s comforting.”

He walked to the model.

Rose watched his expression change.

“Good Lord.”

“You know it?”

“I used to come through here.”

He pointed at the tiny bench.

“There was a crack in that one.”

Rose looked closely.

Elias had included it.

Daniel bent nearer.

“There was a woman at the ticket window.”

“Mara.”

“Yes.”

He turned.

“Is she—”

“No.”

He nodded.

The question settled.

Rose offered tea.

He accepted.

---

Daniel had been a child on the last day.

Not on the train.

On the platform.

His father had come to collect a parcel.

He remembered rain.

A woman telling him not to run.

The smell of wet wool.

He did not remember Mara’s face.

He remembered her voice.

“She gave me a sweet.”

Rose looked at the dish beside the counter.

“Barley?”

He smiled.

“Yes.”

She moved the dish toward him.

He took one.

For a moment, the years did not disappear.

They became visible.

That was different.

Rose preferred it.

---

Daniel returned with a photograph the following week.

A platform crowded with people.

Taken long before the closure.

Mara was at the edge of the frame, turning away from the camera.

Ada stood beside her.

Neither had known they were being photographed.

Rose held it carefully.

“May we copy it?”

“Of course.”

On the back, in someone’s old handwriting, was a date.

Rose read it.

Then read it again.

The day Mara had first started work.

She looked at the women in the photograph.

One facing the future.

One laughing at something outside the frame.

Neither knew.

Rose was grateful for that.

---

They put the copy beside Ada’s photograph.

Not over it.

Beside it.

The wall was growing full.

Imogen suggested a new arrangement.

Rose objected automatically.

Then stopped.

“Show me.”

They moved the frames.

The photographs remained.

The wall looked better.

Change, Rose had learned, did not always arrive carrying a threat.

Sometimes it carried a tape measure.

---

That night, Leah called.

“I’ve had an idea.”

Rose looked at the ceiling.

“How large?”

“Moderate.”

“How expensive?”

“Less than you think.”

“How much less?”

Leah laughed.

She wanted to build a small working bell into the model.

Not loud.

A note you could hear only when the cabinet was open.

Rose considered.

“Why?”

“Because it should have one.”

This was not a technical argument.

It was persuasive.

“Show me the plan.”

---

The little bell took four attempts.

The first sounded like a spoon striking a cup.

The second made no sound.

The third was too loud.

The fourth rang clearly.

A small note.

Not an imitation of the station bell.

A relative.

They fitted it above the model door.

When the cabinet opened, a hidden lever moved.

The bell rang once.

Ada laughed.

Rose closed the cabinet.

Opened it again.

The note sounded.

Leah looked pleased.

“Too much?”

Rose thought of Mara.

The blue notebook.

The yellow sign.

The bus.

“No,” she said. “Just enough.”

---

The next visitor to hear it was a woman who had come in from the rain.

She paused beside the cabinet.

“Oh.”

Rose looked up.

The woman smiled.

“That’s lovely.”

She watched the little station for a moment.

Then turned.

“Do you know when the last bus goes?”

Rose reached for the timetable.

“Yes,” she said. “Let’s check.” 
---

There were two last buses, depending on where the woman meant to end up.

This was not immediately clear because she named the place she called home, then the place printed on her ticket, then the village where someone would collect her.

Rose drew a line on scrap paper.

“Start here.”

The woman pointed.

Rose drew another line.

“And then?”

Another point.

By the time the route was clear, the woman had begun to laugh.

“I make it sound complicated.”

“It is complicated.”

“I suppose.”

“That doesn’t mean you’re doing it wrong.”

The woman folded the paper.

“I should remember that.”

Rose did not offer to print it on a mug.

Some lessons had been learned.

---

Afterward, she put the scrap-paper route in the recycling.

The drawing had served its purpose.

Not everything needed preserving.

She found this increasingly comforting.

The notebook could hold some things.

The archive others.

The rest could happen and pass.

A cup of tea.

A repaired wheel.

A person reaching the right bus.

Enough.

---

In the square, the evening lights came on.

The waiting room windows reflected them.

From outside, the model was too small to see clearly.

Only a warm patch near the counter.

A passerby might glance in and think people were still waiting for a train.

They would be wrong.

They would not be entirely wrong.

Rose stood from the bench and went to close the side window.

It resisted.

She fetched the blade.

There was a way to do this without forcing it.

She had learned.

---

The blade slid beneath the edge.

A little pressure.

A pause.

Then the frame moved.

Rose closed it gently.

Outside, the stream continued without her hearing it.

She knew it was there.

She turned off the lamp.

At the door, she checked her pocket.

Key.

Phone.

Notebook.

One barley sweet.

She stepped into the square.

The bell rang behind her.

She locked the door.

---

Across the street, a child was watching a bus pull away.

He stamped one foot.

His father put a hand on his shoulder.

They stood for a moment.

Then the father pointed toward the library.

The child shook his head.

The father waited.

Rose crossed the square.

“Missed it?”

“Yes,” the father said.

“Where are you going?”

He told her.

Rose looked at the time.

“There’s another route.”

The child looked up.

“Will it take longer?”

“Yes.”

“Will we still get there?”

Rose considered the connection.

Then nodded.

“If you leave now.”

She showed them.

They thanked her.

The child began walking before the explanation was finished.

His father hurried after him.

Rose watched them go.

Then she turned toward home.

---

At the corner, she stopped.

Above the rooftops, the mountain was dark against the sky.

For much of Mara’s life, it had been a place where something had ended.

For Rose, it had become a place beyond which other people lived.

Both were true.

She did not write that down.

Some thoughts could remain thoughts.

She walked on.

---

The next page of the green notebook stayed blank for a week.

Then two.

Rose was busy.

The waiting room needed a new boiler.

A volunteer was ill.

The path flooded near the culvert.

There were calls, forms, tools, tea.

She began to feel guilty about not writing.

Then she remembered the note in Mara’s handwriting.

If you are reading everything in this drawer, you are avoiding something.

She wondered what the reverse might say.

If you are writing everything down, are you avoiding living it?

She closed the notebook.

Went to the station.

Helped move sandbags.

---

The flood receded.

Mud remained.

People arrived with brushes.

Someone brought a pressure washer and was politely prevented from using it on the old woodwork.

The floor was cleaned.

The platform dried.

The model had been moved upstairs at the library in plenty of time.

When it returned, the little bell rang.

Rose stood beside it.

The real room smelled of damp.

The tiny one looked untouched.

For a moment, this annoyed her.

Then she laughed.

Even models were allowed some good luck.

---

At the reopening, Toby brought a cake.

He had baked it himself.

It leaned.

The icing read:

STILL HERE.

Rose looked at it for a long time.

Toby shifted.

“Is that all right?”

“Yes.”

“The letters went wrong.”

“They’re clear.”

“It’s lemon.”

“Even better.”

They cut it.

The cake tasted excellent.

Sound workmanship, Rose thought.

She said it aloud.

Toby smiled.

---

Later, while washing the plates, Ada asked Rose whether the waiting room would always be there.

Rose looked at the old walls.

“No.”

Ada stopped.

“Really?”

“Not always.”

“How long?”

“I don’t know.”

Ada glanced toward the model.

Rose dried a plate.

“We can look after it now.”

Ada nodded slowly.

The answer was smaller than forever.

It was large enough to require work.

They continued washing.

---

That winter, the green notebook reached its final page.

Rose did not notice until she turned and found cardboard.

She sat back.

Outside, snow was beginning.

Inside, the radiator clicked.

She read the last sentence she had written.

Ada said we can look after it now.

Rose had misremembered.

She had said it.

Ada had agreed.

She considered correcting the sentence.

Then she did.

Accuracy mattered.

The page now read:

I said we can look after it now.

Ada nodded.

Rose closed the notebook.

Then she opened it again and added:

That seemed enough to be getting on with.

---

She bought another notebook.

Red cover this time.

On the first page, she wrote nothing.

She put it on the shelf.

There was no need to begin before something happened.

Outside, snow gathered along the platform wall.

The waiting room opened at ten.

Rose took her coat from the hook.

In the pocket, she found the station key.

Heavy.

Familiar.

She closed her hand around it and went to see what the morning required. 
---

The morning required a shovel.

Imogen was standing at the entrance, looking at the snow with an expression that had not yet decided whether it was delight or inconvenience.

“Beautiful,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Slippery.”

“Also.”

They cleared a path from the square.

A passerby joined them.

Then another.

Nobody arranged this.

The snow moved.

The stones appeared.

By ten, there was a narrow safe way to the door.

Rose put out the yellow sign.

Inside, the kettle boiled.

The first visitor came in carrying a bag of books.

“Still open?”

Rose stepped aside.

“Yes,” she said. “Come in.” 
---

The books were mostly atlases.

Old ones.

Countries with names that had changed.

Roads that no longer connected.

Railway lines marked with great assurance through places where grass now grew.

Rose opened one at the county map.

There was Larkspur.

A small black square.

A line crossing the page.

She traced it toward Bellwether.

The town was there too.

Not erased.

Not swallowed by the tunnel.

A place with streets and a river and a school someone had once printed in very small type.

Rose stared.

She had known this, of course.

Yet the name had become so bound to the story that she had almost forgotten it belonged to somewhere people still bought bread.

She looked up.

“What?” Imogen asked.

“I think I should go.”

“Where?”

Rose turned the book.

“Bellwether.”

---

The journey was possible.

Not simple.

A train from the next town, a change at the junction, then a bus around the mountain.

Three hours and forty minutes, if the connections worked.

Leah offered to drive.

Rose declined.

“Why?”

“I want to know the way.”

“You’ll know the road.”

“I want to know this way.”

Leah considered.

Then nodded.

“Take a charger.”

“I will.”

“And food.”

“Yes.”

“And—”

Rose looked at her.

Leah smiled.

“An expanding enterprise.”

---

Rose left on a Wednesday.

She took a small blue bag.

Not Mara’s.

That had finally worn through at one handle.

The new one was lighter and less dignified.

At the station in the next town, she bought a ticket from a machine.

The paper came out warm.

She held it for a moment.

Then found the platform.

The train arrived three minutes late.

No one considered this remarkable.

Rose got on.

---

The railway ran through fields she had seen only from roads.

Back gardens.

Sheds.

The backs of factories.

Laundry.

A football caught in a hedge.

The world had an unpresentable side.

Rose liked it.

At the junction, she had twenty-two minutes.

She bought tea.

The cup was too hot.

At the bus station, she had eleven.

Enough.

The bus climbed.

The mountain appeared through the front window, closer than Rose was used to seeing it.

Then the road turned, and Larkspur disappeared behind stone.

---

Bellwether was larger than she expected.

This was embarrassing.

She had no sensible reason to expect it to be small.

The bus arrived beside a library.

Of course.

Rose stepped down into a square with a fountain that had been switched off for winter.

A bakery stood on the corner.

A woman was arguing with a delivery driver about crates.

For a moment, Rose felt she had gone nowhere and everywhere.

She took out the old atlas page she had copied.

The railway station had been east of the square.

She began walking.

---

The station building was a dental practice.

Rose stood across the street and laughed.

Not unkindly.

There was a sign advertising appointments.

The old canopy had been removed.

The windows were familiar in proportion, unfamiliar in use.

A man came out holding one side of his jaw.

He did not look as though he had completed a mystical journey.

Rose let him pass.

Then she went inside.

---

The receptionist looked up.

“Appointment?”

“No. I’m sorry. I’ve come about the building.”

“Are you from the surveyors?”

“No.”

Rose explained.

Not the train.

The railway.

The waiting room.

The model.

The receptionist listened.

Then she called someone.

A dentist emerged wearing blue gloves and a puzzled expression.

“My grandfather worked here,” he said.

“On the railway?”

“Yes.”

They looked at each other.

Rose had learned to distrust the impulse to call every connection fate.

Still, some connections deserved a chair.

The dentist checked the time.

“I have ten minutes.”

“That’s enough to start.”

---

His name was Arun.

His grandfather had been a porter.

He had not been on duty on the day of the collapse.

For years, this had seemed to the family like luck.

Then Arun had grown old enough to understand that surviving a thing did not always mean escaping it.

“He never liked tunnels afterward.”

Rose nodded.

“I knew someone who worked signals.”

Arun looked at her.

“The warning?”

“Yes.”

He was quiet.

Then he said, “My grandfather talked about him.”

Rose’s hands tightened around the paper cup the receptionist had given her.

“What did he say?”

“That he went back.”

Rose looked down.

Arun waited.

“He did,” she said.

---

Arun showed her a photograph in the hallway.

Bellwether station before the closure.

A porter beside a luggage trolley.

Passengers under the canopy.

A train.

Rose studied the carriages.

The dent in the second one was visible.

A small mark.

Ordinary.

Real.

She had seen a drawing of it in Mara’s notebook.

Now it was here in silver and black.

Arun watched her.

“Would you like a copy?”

“Yes.”

He went to ask the receptionist.

Rose remained before the photograph.

She did not expect anyone to wave.

Still, she looked carefully at every window.

---

Behind the dental practice, a short section of platform remained.

It was used for bins and deliveries.

Arun apologized.

Rose shook her head.

“It’s still a place where things happen.”

He smiled.

The track bed beyond had become a cycle route.

People passed in bright jackets.

A child on a small bicycle rang his bell with enthusiasm disproportionate to the danger.

Rose stepped aside.

At the far end of the platform, grass grew through the stone.

She stood there.

On the other side of the mountain was Larkspur.

Not impossibly far.

Far enough.

---

She ate lunch in the bakery.

The bread was good.

She bought a second roll for the journey home.

Then she found the memorial.

It stood beside the river, not in the square.

The same names.

A different arrangement.

Rose read them.

Ada’s was halfway down.

Elias’s was not there.

Of course it was not.

She had known.

Still, she looked.

She placed a small bunch of yellow flowers beneath the stone.

The florist had asked what they were for.

Rose had said, “Someone.”

That was sufficient for the sale.

---

Before leaving, she telephoned Nell.

“I’m in Bellwether.”

There was a silence.

Then, “How is it?”

Rose looked across the river.

A woman was feeding ducks despite a sign.

“Ordinary.”

Nell breathed out.

“That’s good.”

“Yes.”

“Did you find the station?”

“It’s a dentist.”

Nell laughed.

Rose joined her.

For a moment, the distance between them felt like a room they were both inside.

Then a duck made a furious noise.

Nell asked what that was.

Rose explained.

The conversation continued.

---

The return connections did not work.

The bus was late.

The train had gone.

Rose stood at the junction and looked at the board.

Forty-six minutes.

She sat.

Took out the red notebook.

For the first time, she wrote in it.

Bellwether has a dentist where the station used to be.

Then:

The bread is good.

She looked at the page.

It was not the account she had expected to write.

It was the one she had.

She ate the second roll while it was still fresh enough to be worth having carried.

---

When she reached home, it was dark.

A message from Imogen waited.

How did it go?

Rose typed:

I got there.

Then added:

And back.

Imogen replied:

Both useful.

Rose smiled.

She put the bag down.

The house was quiet.

On the table lay the photograph Arun had printed.

She studied it once more.

Then she turned off the kitchen light.

---

At the waiting room on Saturday, she told the others.

She showed them the photograph.

Leah examined the dent in the carriage.

Ada asked about the memorial.

Imogen wanted to know whether Bellwether had a similar community space.

Rose did not know.

They looked.

It did.

Not in the station.

In an old post office.

Tea.

Books.

A repair group.

Opening hours more ambitious than theirs.

Rose stared at the screen.

Then laughed.

Of course.

---

They wrote a letter.

Not formal.

Not proposing an exchange program, a partnership framework, or any other phrase likely to require a new committee.

Just a letter.

Hello.

We think we have something in common.

A reply came two weeks later.

You may need to be more specific. We also have damp.

Rose read it aloud.

The room laughed.

They had begun well.

---

In summer, a group from Bellwether came over the mountain.

By bus.

They brought cakes.

Larkspur provided lunch.

There were introductions.

Comparisons of insurance.

A vigorous discussion about donated books no one wanted.

Then someone from Bellwether noticed the model.

The room quieted.

Rose opened the cabinet.

The tiny bell rang.

The visitors leaned closer.

One woman touched the glass.

“My mother remembered this train.”

Rose looked at her.

The woman smiled sadly.

“She remembered everything, apparently. Except where she put her glasses.”

People laughed softly.

The story had room for that too.

---

They walked the path together.

At the signal bench, the two groups divided naturally into those who wanted to continue and those who wanted to sit.

No town had a monopoly on either.

Rose sat beside a man named Peter from the old post office.

He looked at Jonah’s plaque.

“Good wording.”

“Yes.”

“Hard to keep things working.”

“Yes.”

He rested his hands on his knees.

“Worth trying.”

Rose smiled.

She did not write it down.

He probably did not want to be quoted.

---

That autumn, Leah began a second model.

Bellwether station.

Before Rose could object, Arun sent photographs.

Peter found old plans.

Ada researched the paint.

The project grew.

There would be no tunnel connecting the models.

Leah was firm about this.

“Too much wall.”

Rose suspected other reasons.

She did not ask.

Instead, they placed the two stations on opposite sides of the cabinet, with the mountain suggested between them in wood and shadow.

No train ran.

Paths emerged from both sides.

At the center, hidden behind a low ridge, was a small light.

Leah said it was only there to balance the scene.

Rose let her.

---

When the second station was unveiled, someone asked why the mountain was not more accurate.

Leah looked at the rough wooden form.

“Because it isn’t a map.”

“What is it?”

Leah considered.

Rose waited.

At last Leah said, “A way of looking.”

The person nodded.

Rose leaned close.

“Sounded wise.”

Leah shut her eyes.

“Damn.”

---

The little light between the stations failed after six months.

For once, Leah was not there to repair it.

Rose opened the cabinet.

The tiny bell rang.

She removed the ridge carefully and found the wire.

Loose connection.

Nothing more.

She fixed it.

The light returned.

No revelation.

No train.

Just a circuit doing what it was meant to do.

Rose sat back.

There was satisfaction in that too.

---

The waiting room clock struck twelve.

Imogen called from the counter.

“Lunch?”

Rose looked at the model.

The two stations.

The path.

The bench.

The little windows lit from within.

“Yes,” she said.

She closed the cabinet.

The bell gave its small clear note.

Then she went to join the others.

---

Outside, the square was full of ordinary arrivals.

A parcel van.

A bicycle.

A bus.

Someone late for an appointment.

Someone early enough to have time for a walk.

A child holding a box with both hands.

Rose saw the box through the window.

She put down her sandwich.

Imogen followed her gaze.

“Bird?”

“No idea.”

The child approached the door.

Knocked.

Rose stood.

“You can come in,” she called.

The latch lifted.

The bell rang.

And there was, as usual, something more to find out. 
---

The box contained a clock.

This disappointed Imogen.

She admitted it immediately.

The child looked offended.

“It’s my grandad’s.”

“I’m sorry,” she said. “I was hoping for a bird.”

“Why would there be a bird?”

Rose took the box.

“It’s happened before.”

The clock was small, square, and missing one hand.

It ticked furiously.

The child’s grandfather came in behind him.

“I told him you weren’t a shop.”

“We aren’t.”

“He insisted.”

The child crossed his arms.

“They fix things.”

Rose looked at the clock.

Sometimes a reputation was simply a list of tasks waiting to happen.

“What’s wrong with it?”

The grandfather laughed.

“Other than the obvious?”

“Yes.”

“It gains an hour a day.”

Rose raised her eyebrows.

“Ambitious.”

---

Leah examined it the following week.

She found three problems.

The missing hand was the least important.

“Can you fix it?” the child asked.

“Probably.”

“Will it be right?”

“Probably.”

“How probably?”

Leah looked at Rose.

Rose smiled into her tea.

“Useful question,” she said.

Leah sighed.

“Bring it back next Saturday.”

The child nodded.

This was a timetable.

He understood timetables.

---

The clock returned to correct time.

Mostly.

It lost twenty seconds a day.

Leah explained this.

The child seemed disappointed.

His grandfather did not.

“Better than losing an afternoon every week to winding it back.”

The child considered.

“Could you make it perfect?”

Leah looked at the old mechanism.

“No.”

“Why?”

“Because it’s this clock.”

The child stared.

Then nodded slowly.

He took the box.

Rose watched them leave.

She had the feeling something had been repaired that was not entirely inside it.

She did not say so.

---

That evening, she wrote in the red notebook.

The clock lost twenty seconds a day.

She stopped.

Then added:

They took it home.

No more was needed.

---

The waiting room grew quieter as winter approached.

Not empty.

Quieter.

People lingered longer.

The light changed early.

The model windows seemed brighter against the dark glass.

One Saturday, Rose sat beside the counter and realized she had spent half an hour simply watching.

A person choosing a book.

A person charging a phone.

A person reading a bus timetable with more concentration than the timetable deserved.

The room was doing its work without requiring her attention at every point.

This should have pleased her.

It did.

It also hurt.

She smiled at the familiar combination.

Mara, she thought, you might have warned me.

Then she remembered.

Mara had.

In a hundred small ways.

---

At closing, Ada took the key.

“I’ll do it.”

Rose hesitated.

Then let go.

The key passed from one hand to another.

Nothing dramatic happened.

The lock turned.

The door held.

Rose put her hands in her pockets.

Ada checked the handle.

“Done.”

They walked across the square.

Behind them, the waiting room stood dark and sound beneath the repaired roof.

At the library shelter, a bus was arriving.

Ada looked at Rose.

“Fancy a ride?”

“Where?”

Ada smiled.

“We can find out.”

Rose considered the time.

Then the weather.

Then the list of things waiting at home.

Then she stepped toward the bus.

“Yes,” she said.

---

The driver recognized them.

“Where to?”

Ada looked at Rose.

Rose looked at the route map.

Then at the dark window, where the square reflected over distant fields.

“Two to the end,” she said.

The driver printed the tickets.

They were warm.

They were light.

Ada took one.

Rose took the other.

They sat beside each other as the bus pulled away.

For a while, neither spoke.

Then Ada said, “Do you think there’ll be somewhere to get chips?”

Rose laughed.

“I should hope so.”

---

At the end of the route there was a village, a closed shop, and a pub with a sign promising food.

The bus would return in fifty minutes.

They went inside.

The chips were excellent.

The table wobbled.

Rose folded a paper napkin and put it beneath the short leg.

Ada watched.

“Always fixing things.”

“Only when I’m eating.”

They sat by the window.

Outside, the bus driver stood beneath the shelter drinking something from a flask.

The village clock struck seven.

It was six fifty-four.

Rose smiled.

Ada asked why.

“Nothing,” she said.

Then, “Everything’s running about as expected.”

---

On the return journey, Ada slept against the window.

Rose watched the road.

Fields.

Lights.

A garage.

A river.

The world had continued rearranging itself.

She no longer minded that it did so without consulting her.

Not always.

At Larkspur, she woke Ada gently.

They stepped down outside the library.

The square was quiet.

The station stood at the far end.

Ada stretched.

“Good idea.”

“Yes.”

“Again sometime?”

“Yes.”

They parted at the corner.

Rose went home.

---

She did not write about the bus ride immediately.

Some things needed to remain warm before being made into sentences.

She made tea.

Opened the kitchen window.

Listened to the street.

Then, after a while, she took out the red notebook.

She wrote:

We went to the end and came back.

She looked at the sentence.

It was enough.

For now.

---

The next morning, snow began before dawn.

Rose watched it from bed.

There would be paths to clear.

Messages to send.

Perhaps the waiting room would open late.

Perhaps it would not open at all.

The world could wait a little.

She lay still until the room grew pale.

Then she got up, made tea, and telephoned Ada.

“Morning.”

“Morning.”

“Have you looked outside?”

“Yes.”

“What do you think?”

Ada was quiet for a moment.

Then she said, “I think we should put the kettle on first.”

Rose smiled.

“Already done.”

---

They opened at eleven.

Someone had brought soup.

Someone else had brought bread.

The snow kept falling.

The waiting room filled with damp wool and the bright relief of people arriving somewhere warm.

At the model, the windows glowed.

The little clocks ticked at slightly different rates.

The bell rang when a child opened the cabinet under supervision.

Outside, the path disappeared beneath white.

Inside, there were chairs.

Rose moved one closer to the radiator.

“Sit down,” she told the person beside her.

They did.

---

By afternoon, the snow had stopped.

A pale sun reached the platform.

Rose stood at the window.

Beyond the bend, the cutting was still.

No rails.

No whistle.

No need.

Behind her, someone was telling a story.

Someone was disagreeing with it.

Someone was asking for more tea.

She turned from the window and went to help.

---

The person who wanted tea was the clockmaker’s grandson.

The clock, he reported, had lost four minutes.

“Since when?”

“Christmas.”

“That’s good.”

“It’s wrong.”

“A little.”

He thought about this.

Then he said, “Grandad says it’s our wrong clock.”

Rose smiled.

“That sounds right.”

The child looked suspicious.

She poured the tea.

Some ambiguities could be left standing.

---

At closing, she checked the station clock.

Three minutes slow.

She left it until Saturday.

Outside, the snow had begun to thaw.

Water ran along the edge of the square.

The library steps shone.

A bus passed.

Rose watched it go.

Then she took the key from Ada and locked the waiting room for the night.

Not every time.

That was the arrangement now.

It worked.

---

When she reached home, a parcel waited beside the door.

From Leah.

Inside was a small wooden figure.

A woman holding a notebook.

The head was slightly too large.

One hand appeared to be holding either a pen or a very narrow umbrella.

Rose laughed.

There was a note.

I thought you should have the opportunity to criticize the workmanship.

Rose turned the figure in her hand.

Then she put it on the kitchen windowsill.

Beside it, the two figures Mara had kept watched the garden.

Three women.

One blue dress.

One cardigan.

One notebook.

The proportions were wrong.

The company was good.

---

Rose telephoned Leah.

“It’s terrible.”

“I know.”

“I love it.”

“I know.”

They talked about the workshop.

The weather.

The little bell.

A new commission.

Nothing that would appear in an archive unless someone became very thorough.

Afterward, Rose stood at the window.

Dark garden.

Warm room.

Three figures on the sill.

She lifted one finger and straightened the smallest.

Then she went to make supper.

---

The following Saturday, a new volunteer arrived.

His name was Ben.

He had filled in the form correctly, provided references, completed the checks, and still looked as though he expected to be told there had been a mistake.

Rose handed him a mug.

“Tea?”

“Yes, please.”

“What would you like to do?”

“Whatever’s useful.”

“A dangerous answer.”

He smiled uncertainly.

Rose showed him the cupboards.

The timetables.

The first-aid kit.

The yellow sign.

Then the kettle.

“This,” she said, “is the easiest part to underestimate.”

Ben nodded seriously.

Rose decided he would probably be all right.

---

By noon, he had made too much tea.

By one, he had stopped apologizing for it.

By two, he was helping someone print a form.

The room absorbed him.

Rose sat for a while beneath the clock.

A new person knowing where the cups were.

A small change.

A future taking up very little space at first.

She closed her eyes.

Not asleep.

Just listening.

---

When she opened them, Ben stood nearby.

“Sorry.”

“You haven’t done anything.”

“I wondered—”

“Yes?”

“The story about the train.”

Rose looked at him.

“Which part?”

“All of it.”

She glanced at the clock.

Then at the room.

There was time.

Not enough for everything.

Enough to begin.

She moved her bag from the chair beside her.

“Sit down,” she said.

---

And because she had learned something from telling it, she began differently this time.

Not with the train.

Not with the ticket.

Not even with the clock.

She began with the question Toby had asked.

“What do you think a station is for?”

Ben looked around.

“To get somewhere else.”

“Yes.”

Rose waited.

He glanced at the people in the room.

“Or to wait.”

“Yes.”

He thought.

“Sometimes just to stop.”

Rose smiled.

“Good. Now.”

She folded her hands around the cup.

“There was a woman named Mara who sold tickets.”

---

Outside, a bus turned into the square.

The bakery opened its back door.

A cyclist leaned a bicycle against the library wall.

The mountain stood beyond the town, neither accusing nor forgiving, simply there.

Inside, the clock was slow.

The tea was hot.

The story had begun again.

And halfway through the first sentence, the bell rang, and someone else came in. 
---

Rose paused.

The newcomer was carrying a ladder.

This was not part of the story.

“Wrong door?” Ben asked.

“Probably not,” Rose said.

The man with the ladder looked embarrassed.

“I was told there was a leak.”

“There was.”

“Still is?”

“No.”

He checked his paper.

The date was three months old.

He looked at Rose.

Rose looked at him.

Then both began to laugh.

“Tea?” she asked.

He set down the ladder.

“Yes, please.”

Ben stood.

“I’ll make it.”

Rose watched him find the cups.

Then she turned back to the story, holding the place with one finger against the side of her mug.

---

There were interruptions.

Of course there were.

A timetable fell from the board.

Someone wanted scissors.

The man with the ladder discovered a loose fitting in the ceiling and repaired it before finishing his tea.

Ben asked whether Mara had known Elias.

Rose said no.

Then yes, perhaps, in a way that did not make the account easier.

Ben accepted this.

The room went on around them.

By the time Ada reached the platform in the story, the real afternoon had moved across the windows.

A line of light touched the photograph on the wall.

Rose did not point it out.

It was sunlight.

It was also well timed.

Both could be true.

---

When she finished, Ben sat quietly.

Then he said, “What happened to Mara?”

Rose looked at him.

“She lived.”

He waited.

Rose smiled.

“She argued. She went places. She helped make this. She grew old.”

“And then?”

“She died.”

Ben nodded.

The answer was not hidden.

It did not need to be.

After a while, he looked around the waiting room.

“She did a lot.”

“Yes,” Rose said. “She did.”

---

At the counter, the man with the ladder was writing his number on a card.

“If the fitting loosens again.”

Rose took it.

“Thank you.”

“No charge.”

“We can pay.”

“I know.”

He lifted the ladder.

At the door, he looked at the model.

“Is that this place?”

“Yes.”

He bent closer.

“Good guttering.”

Rose laughed.

“Tea next time too,” she said.

He smiled and left.

---

The bell settled.

The kettle cooled.

Ben took cups to the sink.

Rose sat for another minute.

There were days when the story felt finished.

There were days when it felt like a door she had only just learned to open.

Most days, it was simply part of the room.

She preferred most days.

Then she stood to help with the washing up.

---

On the way to the sink, she noticed the old penny beneath the short table leg.

Still there.

Still doing its job.

She had forgotten it.

The table had not.

Rose smiled.

No one asked why.

There was enough happening without requiring every smile to explain itself.

---

Evening came.

They stacked chairs.

Checked taps.

Closed windows.

Ben read the closing list twice.

“Is this everything?”

“Usually.”

“What happens if there’s something else?”

“We find out.”

He looked at the model.

The little station glowed.

Rose let him switch it off.

The windows darkened.

The room remained.

At the door, she handed him the yellow sign.

“Cupboard on the left.”

He put it away.

Then they stepped outside.

The bell rang behind them, a clear note in the cooling air. 
---

Rose locked the door.

Ben waited.

“Same time next week?”

“If you’d like.”

“I would.”

“Then yes.”

He walked toward the library shelter.

Rose turned toward home.

At the corner, she glanced back.

Ben stood beneath the bus sign, looking at the timetable.

Then he took out his phone and, after a moment, put it away.

He sat on the bench.

Waiting.

A perfectly ordinary thing.

Rose continued walking.

---

At home, she opened the red notebook.

She wrote:

Ben asked what happened to Mara.

I said she lived.

Then she stopped.

There was no need to explain how many things that answer contained.

She closed the notebook.

In the kitchen, the three small figures stood on the sill.

Beyond them, the garden was dark.

Rose put on the kettle.

There was still evening left. 
---

While the water heated, she washed the cup she had left that morning.

A small ring of tea had dried inside it.

She scrubbed.

The ring lifted.

She set the cup on the draining board.

Outside, someone laughed in the next garden.

A gate clicked.

A child was being called indoors.

Rose opened the window a little.

Cold air entered.

The kettle began to sing.

She turned back toward it, reaching for a clean cup, and the evening continued around her. 
---

She chose the blue cup.

Not because it had belonged to anyone.

Because it was nearest.

This seemed worth noticing.

Then she forgot to notice it and made the tea.

She carried it to the table.

There was a letter to answer.

A bill.

A photograph Leah had sent of the workshop in morning light.

Rose looked at the photograph first.

The workbench was cluttered.

The window was open.

On the sill stood the tiny yellow sign.

The floor, she hoped, was dry.

She wrote a reply on the back of a postcard.

Good light.

Clear a space before you start the next thing.

Then she crossed out the second sentence.

Leah knew how to work.

Rose wrote instead:

I’m glad you’re there.

She found a stamp.

Put the postcard by the door.

Drank her tea.

---

The next morning, she posted it on the way to the waiting room.

The postbox stood beside the library.

Its paint was chipped at the slot.

A small repair for someone.

Rose slipped the card inside and heard it fall.

Then she crossed the square.

The station windows caught the early light.

She took the key from her pocket.

At the door, she paused, not because anything extraordinary had happened, but because the lock sometimes needed to be lifted slightly.

She lifted.

Turned.

The door opened.

Inside, the room was cool.

The clock ticked.

There was a great deal to do.

Rose put down her bag and began. 
---

She started with the chairs.

Then the cups.

Then the window.

At the model, she checked the lights.

Both stations glowed.

The bench remained in shadow.

The tiny bell rang when she opened the cabinet, and she found herself smiling before she had decided to.

She adjusted one figure.

A child near the stream.

Too close to the edge.

Then she stepped back.

Perhaps she was becoming like Mara.

Perhaps everyone became a little like the people who had taught them where to put their hands.

She left the thought unexamined.

The real door was opening.

Ben came in carrying milk.

“I thought we might need it.”

“We do.”

He looked pleased.

Rose took the carton.

“Thank you.”

The kettle went on.

Outside, the first bus of the day turned into the square. 
---

Ben watched it through the window.

“I nearly missed that.”

“But you didn’t.”

“No.”

He took off his coat.

Rose put the milk in the refrigerator.

There were labels to check.

A route change to pin up.

A message from someone asking whether they could bring a broken toaster.

Ben read it aloud.

Rose considered the toaster’s likely condition, the availability of volunteers, the danger of turning the waiting room into a warehouse of appliances abandoned by optimism.

“Ask them to come Thursday.”

Ben typed.

Then he looked up.

“Could it be dangerous?”

“Yes.”

“What do we say?”

“Don’t plug it in.”

He nodded.

A practical beginning.

---

At ten, the waiting room opened.

At ten three, the first person arrived.

At ten seven, someone spilled tea.

At ten nine, the yellow sign stood beside the door.

Rose fetched a cloth.

Ben moved the books.

The person who spilled the tea apologized six times.

On the seventh, Rose said, “You can help by stopping.”

They laughed.

The floor dried.

The morning went on.

---

At eleven, a woman came looking for somewhere to sit until an appointment.

At eleven fifteen, a man came to find out whether the path was muddy.

At eleven twenty, a child came to ring the little bell.

At eleven twenty-one, the bell rang again.

And again.

Rose opened the cabinet only halfway.

“That’s enough for now.”

The child looked disappointed.

Rose showed him the tiny clock.

Its hands were moving.

He leaned closer.

For a moment, the bell was forgotten.

There were other things to notice.

---

At noon, sunlight reached the platform.

The room warmed.

Ben opened the side window.

The frame moved easily.

Rose looked up.

“Did you oil it?”

“No.”

“Sand it?”

“No.”

He glanced at the window.

“Was it difficult before?”

“Sometimes.”

He smiled.

“Good day for it, then.”

Rose listened to the stream.

“Yes,” she said.

“A good day.”

---

And because the day was good, though not perfect, she allowed herself to sit.

The chair was not Mara’s old chair.

It was one someone had donated the previous month.

Firm seat.

Arms at a useful height.

A little ugly.

Excellent.

Rose rested her cup on the table.

The old penny held the leg steady.

The clock lost another second.

The kettle cooled.

Somewhere outside, a bird called.

She did not turn to look.

She knew there would be another sound after it, and another after that.

For now, she listened to this one.

---

Across from her, the woman waiting for her appointment closed her book.

“Do you come here often?”

Rose looked at her.

“Yes.”

“It’s nice.”

“It is.”

“Quiet.”

Rose glanced toward the child at the model, the man discussing mud, Ben opening another carton of milk.

“Sometimes.”

The woman smiled.

Then she opened her book again.

Rose drank the last of her tea.

The conversation had reached exactly the right length.

---

Outside, the bus doors opened.

People stepped down.

One looked at the library.

One looked at a phone.

One looked at the station.

Rose saw the hesitation.

The person crossed the square.

Stopped at the door.

Read the sign.

Then came in.

The bell rang.

Ben turned.

“Morning.”

The person took off a hat.

“Is there somewhere I can ask about getting to Bellwether?”

Rose set down her cup.

“Yes,” she said.

“There is.” 
---

She found the new timetable.

Not the old one.

The one printed last month, already marked with a correction.

The person leaned beside her.

They were traveling to see a sister.

Rose did not make anything of that.

Sisters lived in many places.

Journeys happened every day.

She traced the route.

A train.

A change.

A bus around the mountain.

“There’s time,” she said.

The person smiled.

“That’s good.”

“Yes.”

Rose wrote down the connection.

Then, because it mattered, she added the number of the café by the junction in case the wait became long.

“Keep this.”

They folded the paper.

“Thank you.”

Rose watched them leave.

At the door, they turned back.

“What is this place?”

She looked around.

The answer had changed over the years.

It kept changing.

“A waiting room,” she said.

The person nodded.

Then went to catch the bus. 
---

Ben came to stand beside her.

“Bellwether’s a long way.”

“Longer than it looks.”

“Ever been?”

“Yes.”

“What’s it like?”

Rose smiled.

“It has a good bakery.”

Ben considered this useful information.

Then he went back to the sink.

Rose remained by the window.

The bus pulled away.

The person with the folded timetable sat halfway down, face turned toward the glass.

For a moment, Rose could see them clearly.

Then the bus turned.

The square opened again.

Someone was approaching with a broken toaster.

“Thursday,” Rose murmured.

She went to open the door anyway. 
---

The toaster was not broken.

The plug fuse had failed.

This was discovered after several unnecessary speculations and one cup of tea.

The owner looked embarrassed.

Rose replaced the fuse.

Ben wrote the date on a small label.

The toaster worked.

“Do you want it tested properly on Thursday?” Rose asked.

“Yes, please.”

Sensible.

They put it on the shelf reserved for repairs.

The shelf was already full.

Rose looked at it.

Then at Ben.

“We need a rule.”

He nodded.

“How many things?”

“One per person?”

“How many people?”

Rose thought.

Then sighed.

“This is how committees happen.”

Ben laughed.

---

They made the rule.

It was short.

Bring the item you can carry.

Take it home afterward.

Do not leave it without asking.

Rose read it twice.

Approved.

Ben pinned it above the shelf.

Within an hour, someone asked whether a sewing machine counted as something they could carry if two people helped.

Rose closed her eyes.

Then opened them.

“Let’s see the machine.”

---

There was always something.

Not everything could be repaired.

Not every route connected.

Not every person returned.

The room did not change those facts.

It changed what happened while people met them.

Rose had learned this slowly.

She was still learning it.

At the counter, Ben was listening to someone explain why the sewing machine mattered.

He did not interrupt.

Rose put on the kettle.

The clock ticked.

Outside, the afternoon moved toward another opening of the door. 
---

When the door opened, cold air crossed the floor.

A loose paper lifted.

The bus timetable fluttered against the board.

Rose reached to pin it more securely.

The person entering apologized for the draft.

“No need,” she said.

They carried a narrow parcel wrapped in brown paper.

“Is this the place with the model station?”

“Yes.”

“My aunt said you might like this.”

Rose cleared space on the counter.

The paper came away.

Inside was a painted sign.

LARKSPUR.

Old wood.

Faded letters.

One corner missing.

Ben came closer.

“Where was it?”

“In her shed.”

“Why?”

The person shrugged.

“Apparently it was a very useful shelf.”

Rose laughed.

History, once again, had been holding something up.

---

They found a place for the sign above the books.

Not immediately.

There were measurements.

A search for the right fixings.

A disagreement about whether the wall could take the weight.

Rose was pleased that the disagreement happened before drilling.

At last, the sign hung level.

LARKSPUR.

People looked up as they entered.

Some smiled.

Some did not notice.

Both responses were acceptable.

The sign had spent years as a shelf.

It could tolerate being ignored.

---

At closing, Rose stood beneath it.

The wood smelled faintly of the shed it had left.

Dry earth.

Old paint.

A trace of oil.

She thought of all the things that survived because someone had found another use for them.

Stations.

Papers.

Furniture.

People were not objects.

Still, there was something there.

She did not chase it.

Ada was waiting with the key.

“Ready?”

Rose took one last look.

“Yes.”

They stepped outside.

---

The square was blue with evening.

The station windows darkened.

At the bus shelter, someone waited alone.

Ada looked toward them.

“Do we know when the next one comes?”

“Seventeen minutes.”

“Cold.”

“Yes.”

They crossed over.

Rose asked whether the person wanted to wait inside.

The person looked toward the station.

“Isn’t it closed?”

Rose glanced at Ada.

Ada lifted the key.

“Not necessarily.”

They walked back together.

---

The room warmed slowly.

No need to turn everything on.

One lamp.

The kettle.

A chair near the radiator.

The person’s name was Julia.

She was on her way home from a hospital visit.

She did not want to talk about it.

Rose did not ask again.

They sat.

Ada checked the bus.

Still seventeen minutes, somehow.

Then fourteen.

Then nine.

Julia held the cup.

At five minutes, she stood.

“Thank you.”

Rose took the cup.

Ada opened the door.

The bus was turning into the square.

Julia stepped out.

Then looked back.

“Good night.”

“Good night.”

They watched her board.

Only then did Ada lock up.

---

On the walk home, Ada said, “We’ll never leave if we do that every time.”

“No.”

“Do we have to?”

“No.”

Ada thought about it.

“Sometimes?”

“Sometimes.”

They reached the corner.

The mountain was invisible in the dark.

The library windows reflected a streetlamp.

Ada put the key in her pocket.

“Good night.”

Rose smiled.

“Good night.”

---

At home, Rose wrote:

We reopened for seventeen minutes.

She paused.

Then added:

It took less time than I expected.

The statement was not accurate in a measurable way.

It was accurate in the way she meant.

She left it.

The red notebook lay open on the table.

There were still many pages.

Outside, a bus passed on its final route.

Rose listened until the engine faded.

Then she turned the page and wrote the next date, ready for whatever came after. 
---

For three days, nothing seemed worth writing.

This did not mean nothing happened.

The sewing machine was repaired.

The toaster passed its test.

The clockmaker’s grandson came in to report that his grandfather had bought a digital clock and disliked it.

Imogen found a supplier for cheaper tea without sacrificing what she called “basic decency.”

Ada applied for a course in conservation.

Ben remembered everyone’s preferred mugs.

The room worked.

Rose did not want to turn every working day into a paragraph.

She let the page remain mostly blank.

On Thursday evening, she wrote one sentence.

The toaster went home.

Then she laughed at herself and left it there.

---

Friday brought a letter from Julia.

Not a long one.

Thank you for opening the door again.

There was no explanation.

No account of the hospital visit.

No need.

Rose put it in the folder for letters.

Then she made a copy for Ada.

Ada read it.

For a while, she was quiet.

Then she said, “Seventeen minutes.”

Rose nodded.

They went back to work.

---

The waiting room’s roof needed cleaning.

The gutters were full of leaves.

Ben volunteered.

Rose refused to let him climb without proper equipment.

He looked offended for half a second, then sensible.

They hired someone.

Not everything had to be done by goodwill on a ladder.

The man who came recognized the old sign.

“My uncle had one of those.”

“As a shelf?” Rose asked.

He stared.

“Yes.”

They laughed.

The guttering was cleared.

Water ran where it was meant to run.

A modest miracle, if anyone insisted on calling it one.

Rose did not.

---

That evening, rain came hard.

She stood inside the waiting room and watched it pour from the downpipe.

No overflow.

No drip near the window.

No spreading stain on the ceiling.

She telephoned Leah.

“What?”

“The gutters work.”

Leah was silent.

Then she said, “I’m very happy for you.”

“You should be.”

“I am.”

Rose laughed.

It was good to have someone who understood the triumph without requiring it to become larger.

---

At the model, the gutters had never failed.

Rose looked at them.

Perhaps Leah should add leaves.

Then she decided not to ask.

Some small worlds could remain easier.

For now.

---

The next morning, the first frost returned.

Rose arrived early.

The key resisted.

She lifted the latch slightly.

Turned.

The door opened.

Inside, the waiting room held the night’s cold.

She switched on the lights.

The model glowed.

The clock ticked.

She put on the kettle.

Then she took the yellow sign from the cupboard and set it beside the entrance.

There might be ice on people’s shoes.

There was no reason not to be ready.

---

Beyond the platform, grass silvered beneath the sky.

The old line curved out of sight.

Rose stood at the door for a moment.

A rail might have settled under weight.

A whistle might have sounded.

Nothing did.

A bakery van rolled past the square.

The driver raised a hand.

Rose raised hers.

Then the kettle clicked.

She went inside.

---

At ten, Ben arrived.

At ten five, Ada.

At ten twelve, the clockmaker’s grandson with his grandfather.

At ten fifteen, someone who only wanted directions.

At ten twenty, someone who wanted directions and did not leave for an hour.

The room filled gradually.

The cold lifted.

The window clouded.

Rose wiped a clear patch in the glass.

Outside, a bus was approaching.

She could see faces behind the windows.

Arriving.

Passing through.

Going somewhere else.

She turned back as the door opened.

“Morning,” she said.

The person entering smiled.

“Am I early?”

Rose looked at the clock.

Then at the kettle.

Then at the empty chair beside the radiator.

“No,” she said. “You’re here.” 
---

The person laughed.

“That sounds philosophical.”

“It wasn’t meant to.”

“Good.”

They took off their coat.

Rose was relieved.

There were enough thoughts in the room without adding one to every greeting.

She poured tea.

The person sat.

Outside, the bus moved on.

Inside, Ben found another cup.

Ada opened the model cabinet for a child.

The tiny bell rang.

The real clock ticked.

Rose carried the tea to the table, set it down carefully, and stayed a moment to hear what the visitor had come to ask. 
---

It was a small question.

Where could they buy a button?

Not a packet.

One button.

For a coat they were wearing, which had lost its middle fastening somewhere between the bus and the square.
